the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. - (fi) mr president, our committee is dealing with these questions from many different angles, and i will begin by talking about the research angle. we are very pleased that the rapporteur has taken into account in his conclusions our proposal that research infrastructures should be extended in the cohesion countries by establishing vocational schools and universities in such a way that they better serve the populations of the less developed regions and enable trained people to remain in their region of origin. this is something that can be achieved by taking action at the level of public authorities. such decentralisation of higher education is undoubtedly a useful policy when it comes to guaranteeing development. the second point we wanted to draw attention to, precisely from the point of view of industrial policy, is that we would have liked the commission to pay more attention to the impact of the growth of services, electronic commerce and the use of the internet when considering the coordination of the structural funds and the cohesion fund. in the past, poverty and wealth were more dependent on the structures of economic life. the rich regions were those where there were industrial jobs, whereas today these regions may well have become a burden and be poor. it is therefore necessary to invest in the new sectors of activity, the so-called 'electronic' production and the production of electronic services, because these are sectors of the future. in my opinion, the committee responsible for drawing up the report has not sufficiently taken this aspect into account in its own report, and that is why, on behalf of the committee on industry, foreign trade, research and energy, i draw the attention of the european commission to this. finally, since our committee deals with energy issues, we would have liked to see even more emphasis on support for renewable energy through the resources of the structural funds and the cohesion fund, which would have allowed, through a process of coordination, to increase the exploitation of renewable forms of energy, so that the lack of funding resources for the energy programme could have been compensated by these larger funds.
- (fi) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to thank mrs schroedter for her excellent report; the commission has given its full attention to this task and, during the attention should be paid to the overseas and other peripheral regions and wants more interaction between the city and the countryside. we oppose the interventionism of the central administration of the union and of the member states and we call for a reduction in the bureaucracy that has become embedded in the development and implementation of programmes. the effectiveness of programmes - (fi) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should first like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent work and also for the way in which the amendments were considered in committee. the sixth periodic report provides a basis for assessing the achievement of the european union's regional policy objectives. the report shows that, despite all efforts, growth is uneven. extremely rapid growth continues in central europe. similarly, the most dynamic centres continue to grow faster than the european average, while many regions in the north and south of europe are experiencing significantly slower growth. a thorough analysis of the reasons why regional policy is not delivering the expected results in all regions is now needed. is it because of the bureaucracy? or have regional disparities - distances, too hot or too cold climate, scattered habitat, harsh conditions - not been sufficiently taken into account? how can the european union meet the challenges of global change so that less developed regions can keep pace? it would also be important to analyse the effects of enlargement on the structural funds and on the development of the outermost regions. the member states also have their share of responsibility in this area and they must not forget this. some member states have contravened the principle of subsidiarity and reduced regional resources at national level, while regional aid provided through the european union has been increasing. this has had a negative effect on the results of regional policy. the future will also require consideration of indicators to target action at the right time and place. for example, the uncontrolled flow of migration has not been sufficiently taken into account. here too, the decisive role of smes as job providers and drivers of regional development should be emphasised. it is particularly important to make new technologies and know-how available to businesses in developing regions. our group supports the adoption of this report.

has recently taken a position. they must of course be regarded as state aid, but i think they are a better solution than direct aid. the report on competition policy underlines the commission's confidence in strict legislation rather than in economic arguments that interfere with competitiveness. the european economy will never reach the level of competitiveness it hopes to achieve unless we are confident in the functioning of the market. if competition policy is subordinated to socio-political and environmental issues, real efficiency and economic growth will remain a dream. competition policy must be seen as a component of the economic whole and assessed in relation to, among other things, commercial policy and intangible rights, and must not only emphasise the social dimension. socio-political objectives will be best achieved through economic growth, not by finding compromises on competition policy. the rapkay report also emphasizes the international dimension of competition. i think it would be good to have an international consensus on certain fundamental principles of competition. instead, the search for minimum standards simply leads to looking at the lowest barriers and finally to the lowest common denominator, which makes competition policy objectives irrelevant.

the commission is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. the commission approved at first reading in november 1999 a code of good administrative behaviour to be included in its regulation. the commission is currently consulting its staff representatives on the document, a process which will be finalised in the coming weeks. the commission will then adopt the code at second reading. it should be noted that the new commission followed up immediately on the european ombudsman's decision of 28 july 1999 in its own-initiative inquiry into the code of administrative conduct. the commission would like to emphasize in particular that it has taken account of all the european ombudsman's draft recommendations in drafting the code. the code will deal exclusively with relations between the commission's administration and the public. it will be adopted by a legally binding commission decision to be published in the official journal of the european communities. the document was drafted taking full account of the provisions contained in the draft prepared by the european ombudsman's office. in accordance with the provisions of the treaties, the commission is itself responsible for drawing up its own rules of procedure. however, it goes without saying that the commission subscribes to the principle of regular political dialogue with the european parliament on all aspects of administrative reform.

- (fi) madam president, although some of the objectives of the tobin tax are particularly interesting, the fact that we are the first to adopt this tax and precisely in the european union would be a fatal blow to the european foreign exchange market. if the other major currency areas of the world were to remain outside the tobin tax, the tax levied in europe would have the effect of transferring the currency market to those areas. this has been made clear on several occasions in this debate. it is not enough for the industrialized countries to participate in this system, because then the foreign exchange market would escape the industrialized countries and take refuge in tax havens. this is why the tax must be adopted at global level; the g7 or other industrialized countries are not enough. the uniform and global adoption of the tax is such a difficult task - and i personally do not think we will succeed in this - that we should rather mourn the tobin tax. moreover, even if there is a global agreement on the adoption of this tax, the question of sharing the proceeds of taxation remains. the few global exchanges would certainly not welcome the fact that tax revenues are used for all sorts of un public interest projects without a significant share of the revenues going to them. as for the usefulness of the tobin tax in preventing future financial crises, it should be noted that the tax does not remove the causes of currency overvaluation. this is why we must focus our attention on the policies - mr karas also spoke about this - which lead to the overvaluation of a currency in relation to its actual potential. but this would require control measures for the import and export of capital, otherwise we cannot concentrate on these policies. the tobin tax therefore seems a good idea, but it is not without problems, even if we were to succeed in implementing it, which i repeat, i doubt. it should be remembered that in the present state of affairs, money is primarily information, and that in the future the world economy will increasingly be based on the power of knowledge and intellectual capital. for this reason, the uninterrupted flow of huge masses of wealth between the world's financial centres is not purely a speculative activity entirely disconnected from the real economy. if this feedback mechanism, which gives users the opportunity to make choices, is impeded by measures such as the tobin tax, this can result in wrong choices with an economic cost far greater than the tax would ever reap. that is why, as mr kalas has already very well explained, our group is absolutely opposed to the adoption of the tobin tax.
- (fi) madam president, ladies and gentlemen, let us look at currency speculation. it produces nothing new. the tax would thus hit exactly those it is intended to hit: speculators. who will defend these speculators when nurses and teachers pay exorbitant taxes? article 73c of the eu treaty provides for the possibility of taxing financial transactions. opponents of the taxation of foreign exchange support tax havens, which are 62 in total. the european union should draw up a tax convention to which any state could join at the outset. the tobin tax, which is low, would not cause major problems of tax evasion. it is strange to say that if not everyone is involved, it cannot be implemented. the european union itself was founded with just a few states at the start, and look where we are today. the truth is that the biggest problem is political: we do not want to tax capital, we want to increase taxation on people. the tax would finally bring a political dimension to the system of global and undemocratic financial power. we must, ladies and gentlemen, challenge the privilege enjoyed by the global speculative elite, which allows them to run the world economy as they please outside the framework of tax systems.
- (fi) mr president, this is a report on a very technical aspect, but one which is nevertheless of great importance for energy savings and also for employment in europe. the rapporteur has done an excellent job of analysing the energy impacts of the directive, but at the same time we have unfortunately lost sight of the fact that if it were to be implemented, the directive would create unemployment in europe, especially in germany, but also in finland, austria, italy and spain. two types of ballasts are used in fluorescent tubes, which have an effect on their energy efficiency. ferromagnetic ballasts produce a lot of light and a lot of heat. in the nordic countries, this heat saves heating energy and is not simply wasted energy. electronic ballasts produce better light and less heat and are significantly more energy efficient than ferromagnetic ballasts. these will be the ballasts of the future, if we can make their price competitive with that of ferromagnetic ballasts. philips is certainly right to invest in this technology. however, it should not be the case that its products become the only european standard by a binding decision taken at political level. mr turmes proposes in his report that ferromagnetic ballasts be banned, contrary to the commission and council. if the report is adopted, the european parliament's position would mean the end of the european industry in the sector. in other parts of the world, ferromagnetic ballasts would be manufactured and used, which are essential in climatic conditions such as those i personally know: in cold and humid conditions, only ferromagnetic ballasts can be used. the ban would shift production of ferromagnetic ballasts from europe to developing countries. we have already experienced this in the united states. if we want to preserve jobs in europe, we cannot adopt the turmes report on the points raised in mr westendorp and cabezan's amendments. the production costs of electronic ballasts are - (fi) madam president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr commissioner, on behalf of the group of the european people's party and the european democrats, i think i can say that we broadly agree with the ideas that you, mr guterres and mr prodi, have put forward here. on behalf of my group, i congratulate the portuguese presidency on the organization of the extraordinary lisbon council and we wish you good luck and success, so that your praiseworthy efforts will be crowned with success. i would like to mention a few of the points which are of particular concern to our group. the key to the well-being of eu citizens is to strive to maintain sustainable economic growth across the eu, to ensure a more equitable distribution of wealth at regional level and to ensure that more people can enjoy a decent income from their work without having to rely on society. the processes initiated by joint decisions aimed at improving employment, strengthening economic renewal and improving social cohesion in the european union are of the utmost importance. it is good that the lisbon council at the end of this month is pausing to see where we are. despite their diversity, these processes must have a single objective: improving the quality of life of the people of the european union. however, no new formulas, working groups or forums are needed to achieve this. the instruments, everybody knows them. but national views, and sometimes also ideological resistance, often prevent these instruments from being exploited. the key now would be to remove as quickly as possible the obstacles to the establishment of a genuine european market area, instead of living, as is still the case in many sectors, in fifteen separate national markets. when the idea of the internal market was seriously advanced in the 1980s, it was still possible to move towards it at the speed of a steam-powered locomotive, so to speak. today, with e-commerce taking off and the speed of data processing and exploitation taking on a whole new dimension, we need at least the speed of mika häkkinen's car. however, we must ensure that the impulse given by the engine to the decision-makers does not stop prematurely, before the goal is reached. how can we imagine being able to establish the most competitive economic zone in the world if we do not have the courage to unleash competition within that zone? the opening up of the telecommunications market to competition, which has already been decided, and the introduction of competition in the energy sector will create much-needed new jobs and bring down prices for industry as well as for consumers. similarly, removing the obstacles to small and medium-sized enterprises - whether it be the complexity of administrative procedures, the acquisition of venture capital or tax issues - is a key element in improving competitiveness and well-being. improving global competitiveness also requires moderate tax harmonisation and modernising our social protection systems so that countries in the european union with high taxation can reduce the tax burden, which is detrimental to employment. however, it is through education and training and research that solid foundations are built. it is a good thing to work towards the rapid establishment of a knowledge infrastructure that individuals, schools and businesses can access at moderate cost or free of charge. in particular, the creation and maintenance of resources in promising employment sectors must be given the attention it deserves. these sectors include, of course, information technology, e-commerce, tourism and, for example, services for the elderly, because it is undeniable that our population is getting older. two things must be emphasized, both by us and by the commission and the council, and we cannot stress them enough. these tasks will remain essentially the responsibility of the member states, whether in education, training and research, improving competition or removing obstacles to entrepreneurship. in some sectors coordination is of course not excluded in the framework of the european union's activities to avoid redundancies, but open and honest competition is even more important, even in some non-commercial sectors. the use of benchmarking and best practice comparison methods advocated by the commission requires a high degree of openness at european level in areas which do not fall under the scope of trade secrets. the group of the european people's party and european democrats stresses its aspirations to develop a social market economy, or, as we now say, an eco-social economy, which takes into account the well-being of society as a whole, including the environment. in pursuing our objective, we stress the importance of the european social model, while stressing that it cannot be a rigid, sclerotic system based on the advantages acquired in society: on the contrary, it must evolve in line with the changes in society. the modernisation of the european social model and the improvement of its efficiency are as important as the corresponding modernisation of the economy. work should be the most natural way to increase the well-being of families, but in cases where this is not possible, a social support network is in place to prevent exclusion. there is an evolution in working methods which may become a problem from the point of view of the harmonious development of human relations: telecommunications, the internet, the computer screens with which we work and television, which now seems outdated, threaten to make the relationship between man and machine the essential element of the information society. what counts, however, are the relationships that develop in the family, in the workplace, in society and in international contacts. as we emphasise the importance of education and training, it would be good to try to raise awareness among our young people as early as possible. the commission, in my view, is quite right to mention the deficits which are hindering employment growth in the european union. among these deficits, the one of know-how is mentioned. in a union of 15 million unemployed, there is already a shortfall of about 1 million workers in sectors where skills are lacking. some propose to import a highly trained workforce into the european union. i ask the question: is this not an unfair treatment of the less developed countries, whose brains we are thus stripping? the un, for its part, has indicated that within two years there will be a shortfall of 40 million workers in europe due to demographic change. it is difficult to pursue the moderate policy, with slow results, which must be pursued now, if we hold up such a prospect of the future to the eyes of europeans, while they themselves fear for their jobs.
- (fi) mr president, the key to success for developing countries is the possibility for them to participate in development themselves, both in defining problems and in finding solutions. having worked in development cooperation myself, i have found that this approach has fortunately in recent years replaced the old paternalistic ideology of assistance, where the so-called 'advanced' countries thought that progress consisted in transferring our way of life to the less developed countries. the new development cooperation model emphasises partnership, where the one providing aid is the party listening to the other. he must have the patience to let the people themselves define their problems and to let them themselves draw up plans to remedy the situation and implement the solution themselves. the main task of those providing aid is to make themselves unnecessary and to ensure that the work generates well-being and activities on the spot. from this point of view, mr wijkman has produced an excellent report, which stresses the importance of this model, the only one that can succeed and be morally sustainable. yet, until now, this approach has been appallingly poorly applied at community level in science and technology projects. the european union's framework programmes do not extend to developing countries. in other words, environmental science and technology projects cannot include partners from, for example, north africa. there is nothing to prevent them from doing research, but there is simply no funding because developing countries cannot be admitted to consortia as a party asking for money. why can 't it be ? the eastern european countries have been given more opportunities to participate! it is even less understandable when we consider how directly we are concerned, for example, with the desertification of north africa or the problems of the mediterranean, and we must give greater responsibility to local researchers in the form of funding, in order to achieve a development which benefits the local people and which leaves behind activities even after the donors have left. we must ensure that not a single project is implemented without the contribution of local researchers.
- (fi) mr president, madam commissioner, the revision of the directive on roadworthiness tests for commercial vehicles is an urgent and important task. the rapporteur has also reached a completely satisfactory position. commercial vehicles cover a very high number of kilometres per year and their technical condition cannot be checked effectively by annual inspections alone. the need for specific technical roadside checks is also due to the fact that heavy goods vehicles from third countries travel on the roads of the european union and are often in poor condition, which poses a serious safety risk. the revision of the directive will significantly increase road safety if its provisions are properly observed and if effective, proportionate and dissuasive penalties can be imposed for infringements. the effectiveness of the directive will be enhanced if member states can agree on the harmonisation of the sanctions regime. however, this should not delay the rapid implementation of the directive. a recent example - this was in finland - shows how urgently the directive needs to be revised. the police and the vehicle inspection authorities recently checked 62 heavy goods vehicles in a punch-out operation in the east of the country. thirty-three of them were fined and 16 were given a warning. three vehicles were completely banned from the road. only thirteen vehicles were above reproach. a considerable proportion of the vehicles in poor condition came from third countries. the most serious case was a polish semi-trailer with a tractor vehicle with no brakes at all, a defective cargo lock and serious brake leakage. i'll let you imagine the danger of such a vehicle on snowy and icy roads. our group supports amendment no 3 and wants the directive to be implemented quickly.

- (fi) mr president, the amendments of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection generally emphasize the high level of environmental protection and for that reason many of them are quite acceptable. infrastructures for waste collection and incineration vary from country to country, however, so that in reality the provisions do not apply equally to all. in finland and some other countries, the sorting of waste close to the place where it is produced is already well advanced. this is why we do not mix waste in incineration, as is done in some countries. mixed waste incineration is a bad environmental policy. the sorted waste can be incinerated by co-incineration with other fuels. the amendments tabled by the committee on the environment unjustifiably lump in the same bag mixed waste incineration and co-incineration. such a practice risks, through indirect economic effects, weakening the level of environmental protection in the countries which sort waste. that is why amendments nos 14, 17, 18 and 27 should not be adopted. in conclusion, i would point out that the cement lobby must not be given a better competitive position than hazardous waste treatment plants themselves.
- (fi) mr president, waste recycling and waste policy as a whole are becoming an increasingly important part of community environmental policy. in order to act rationally and in a timely manner, we must bear in mind something that has already been discussed here, namely the waste hierarchy: prevention, recycling, incineration with energy consumption and final disposal. the commission proposal does not correspond in all respects to this idea, but rather takes as its principle that everything must and can be incinerated. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy has, at second reading, added to the common position the amendments needed to promote the waste hierarchy and i hope that these views will also be taken into account in tomorrow's vote. i also want to see support for the proposal, which was widely approved in committee, to facilitate the co-incineration of waste from the paper and cellulose industries in the same plant. this waste is an important source of energy not only for the industrial plants themselves but also for the surrounding municipalities. since the waste hierarchy is implemented very differently in different member states and since in most countries hazardous and non-hazardous waste is incinerated in the same incinerator, very strict rules are needed. however, in countries where waste recycling is relatively well done, such as finland, and where it is known what is incinerated and where, stricter measurement standards do not improve the state of the environment, and may even weaken it, because incineration of waste becomes impossible in small installations and transport costs increase. from the point of view of reducing air pollutants, it would be essential that derogations from the strict standards be granted only when incineration undertakings can prove beyond doubt that no more pollutants are being released into the atmosphere than permitted by the permits.
- (fi) mr president, the directive on waste incineration is based on a good principle, but in my opinion it was drawn up in too much detail. what concerns me most is the coincidence. the sorted municipal waste and in particular the residual fibrous masses from the wood processing process constitute a good fuel for many heating plants, when added to others. the proposal for a directive calls for continuous, costly, and in some cases impossible and environmentally unnecessary measures for co-incineration plants. the final result is the opposite of what was intended: sorted non-hazardous waste, which is a fuel that can be used by heating plants, will have to be dumped in landfills, where it will increase methane emissions, or else it will have to be transported hundreds of kilometres away, with environmental pollution as a result, to large-scale incineration plants. the report contains many problematic amendments. i think that the matter should be referred back to committee or else the council's common position should be adhered to in its broad outline.
- (fi) mr president, i thank mr blokland for this report, his task was extremely difficult. the development of environmental legislation for europe as a whole is not easy, as the conditions vary widely between member states. i come from a country where a line has already been adopted which corresponds surprisingly closely to the community strategy on waste management. the difference is that in this area finland has managed to establish practices that are already working perfectly well, as you have heard explained tonight. it is therefore regrettable that the same mechanisms which are needed to give guidance to countries which have done less well in waste management are forcing more advanced countries to do less well in this respect. it shouldn't be. mrs paulsen was right: it is precisely now that the principle of subsidiarity should be observed. if the use of a waste fuel is illogically subject to much higher emission standards than other fuels, the exploitation of waste fuels will cease. there is no alternative to incinerating combustible waste: it will end up in landfills. this will increase the formation of methane from the decomposition of organic waste and will increase the greenhouse effect. this is particularly the case for the incineration of non-hazardous sludge from paper and cellulose production. it is therefore important that the european parliament amends the proposal for a directive on this point in the manner set out by the commission in amendment no 4. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy has adopted a number of proposals which, if implemented, would make it more difficult to use co-incineration, which is environmentally justified. in particular, the fuel itself used in co-incineration - and therefore other than waste - is subject to emission limit values identical to those for waste incineration, whereas in heating plants community legislation allows the same fuel to be used with higher limit values. another example is the requirement to continuously measure ammonia levels. it increases the cost of measurement in a fundamental way, without benefit to the environment, because the measurement obligations do not only concern installations where ammonia emissions are likely to occur. the money invested, which does not generate any environmental value, is no longer available for environmentally beneficial investments.
the rapporteur, mr president, has set the european union ambitious environmental objectives, which were set out in the fifth environmental action programme and the 1997 acidification strategy, amongst others. these programmes aim to protect people and nature from the harmful effects of air pollution, so that the limits of the risks to nature and people are not exceeded. the commission's proposal for national emission ceilings is a significant step towards achieving the european union's objectives of protecting citizens from the harmful effects of air pollutants. however, it will take time to correct the effects of the mistakes of the past decades. the technical possibilities for eliminating the sources of polluting emissions are not even fully known. for this reason, it is rational to proceed in stages, as the commission proposes. the first step is to set interim targets for 2010, which means reducing exposure to ozone by two thirds by that year. however, we cannot be satisfied with this, because even if this target is reached, there will still be too many areas where pollution levels exceed the limits from which they are dangerous for man and nature. i therefore proposed in my report that this interim objective be supplemented by a long-term objective and a final objective by 2020. we could then finally reach a situation where critical levels and loads are not exceeded and all individuals are effectively protected against the known health risks of air pollution. i would like to draw your attention to one of my amendments on this subject, where, as a result of a wording error, there is a wrong word in your documents. instead of is not reached, you should read is not exceeded. i hope that this will be taken into account before the vote tomorrow. how, then, can we achieve a situation where air pollutants do not exceed what nature and man can tolerate? the commission proposal sets ceilings for each member state for certain pollutants which are treated together and which are responsible for acidification, ground-level ozone formation and soil eutrophication. it has been shown that tackling these problems and the pollutants that cause them together is the most cost-effective way to influence and control the agents responsible for air pollution. it is the level of ceilings, that is to say the level set for each country, which is the subject of the greatest disagreement in the implementation of the directive. the same pollutant reduction targets as in the present directive were also discussed in the context of the unece convention, and the discussions on this occasion led to the signing of the gothenburg protocol. there is a clear difference between that proposal and the commission's proposal. if we settle for the gothenburg level, we will never reach the targets set by the european union for air pollutants. if we compare the differences between the gothenburg document and the commission proposal in terms of health effects, the level of reduction proposed by the commission saves up to four thousand lives a year in europe. although it has been conclusively demonstrated that ambitious environmental targets are a component of the health and safety of individuals, a lower level of target is justified by the fact that cleaner air is too costly for industry and society. however, it must be said that the costs in this area have been greatly exaggerated. when the benefits of better air quality for people, nature and buildings are taken into account, the result is that the economic benefits are four times higher than the costs. it would also be possible to revise the proposal for a directive to take better account of the situation in the candidate countries and of the issues concerning the countries in the european union's neighbourhood.

the rapporteur. -en) mr president, the two directives on the supervision of the activities of electronic money institutions are of the utmost importance for the development of electronic commerce in the european union. the aim was to ensure the financial integrity of electronic money institutions and to help create an environment conducive to the expansion of these new means of payment. since many of the amendments proposed by parliament at first reading have been taken into account, i personally feel that parliament's opinion is sufficiently reflected in the common position. the common position reflects a compromise reached after lengthy negotiations in the council. i am well aware that any amendment to the council's common position runs a real risk of the directive being rejected in conciliation, since the directive was, and still is, a very delicate compromise. as rapporteur, i have tried to avoid the directive being subject to the conciliation procedure. i have put forward a compromise proposal which, unfortunately, was not accepted by my group. i do not think we can reach a conclusion which would serve the interests of all the parties concerned better than this common position does. the question for us in the european parliament is therefore whether the proposed amendments are absolutely necessary, compared with the potential risk that the control framework will not be adopted at all. this would mean that the situation would remain uncertain for consumers. i would also like to remind my colleagues that the amendments now proposed are new and therefore not based on the position adopted by parliament at first reading. these are amendments proposed by the ecb which were unanimously rejected by the council. i also fear that by emphasizing the european central bank's position on monetary stability control, we are causing serious problems for the member states outside the euro area. we must not forget that the ecb has no veto power over community legislation. the ecb's control of monetary stability is based on the ec's founding treaties and should not be reinforced by provisions contained in ec directives. amendments nos 1 and 2 add the words 'at face value' to the report. these additional words are not absolutely necessary, since article 3 sets out the conditions for reimbursability. this article implies that the repayment will be at face value and without any costs other than those strictly necessary for the execution of the operation. in the latter case, the additional words would add nothing and mislead the consumer. the addition of the words 'at face value' to article 3 (1) could mislead the consumer, since it is always possible to deduct the costs which are strictly necessary for the execution of this operation. the common position provides that member states may authorise their competent authorities to grant exemptions from the application of some or all of the provisions of the proposed directive. the exemption is possible in cases where the total amount of electronic money issued is limited and where the currency issued is accepted as a means of payment by only a limited number of businesses. for example, the exemption could be used for university campus payment cards or for local transport systems. in all such cases, the amount stored shall not exceed a maximum of eur 150. in addition, exempted systems will not benefit from the provisions of the single market passport. the activities of these limited national systems cannot, from the point of view of financial stability, be compared with the activities of large electronic trading systems or large credit institutions. it is unlikely that their effects will spread to the extent of endangering the stability of the financial market. moreover, these limited national systems will not escape scrutiny, since the text states that any exemption must be decided on an individual basis by the supervisory authorities. the exempted establishments will therefore be registered and supervised by the supervisory authorities. amendments nos 3, 4 and 5 reduce the scope of the exemption. it was clear to the council that the price to pay for a directive was that the exemption option should be included. the council was able to adopt its common position only because it thought that a directive which included an exemption but tightened up the rules and allowed competition in a single market was better than no directive at all. the commission has adopted a similar view. the sooner the directive is adopted and applied, the sooner the effects of the improved regulation will be felt. if this still raises concerns in practice, then the clause on revision of article 11 remains reserved. mr president, at the moment there is no requirement to monitor national electronic money systems. i therefore do not think that the european parliament should adopt any amendment, otherwise the whole directive will be overturned.

counsel. - (pt) in 1998, the european parliament and the council adopted the fifth framework programme for research and technological development, which runs from 1998 to 2002. one of the characteristics of the framework programme is that it contains a set of thematic programmes, which define priority research areas and horizontal themes. one of these themes is entitled 'affirming the international role in community research', and its objective is to promote scientific and technological cooperation at international level and to contribute to the implementation of the community's external policy in this field. the general objectives of scientific cooperation at international level are also affirmed in this framework programme, including support for the development of a europe-mediterranean partnership and contribution to economic, social and scientific development in developing countries. in december 1998, the council adopted rules on participation for enterprises, research centres and universities, and rules on the dissemination of research results for the implementation of the fifth framework programme. as regards the participation of legal entities from third countries and international organisations, all indirect research activities under the thematic programmes shall be open to research entities from mediterranean partner countries and developing countries, in principle without community funding - the only exception being situations where community funding is considered to be absolutely vital for the objectives of the activity. article 6 authorises the participation of third country entities in specific research and technological development activities covered by this specific programme on the international role of community research. in january last year the council adopted the specific programmes of the fifth framework programme, which, as you know, specifies the objectives and activities of the various categories of third countries. as regards the mediterranean countries, an indicative amount of eur 55 million was allocated to research and development activities, such as integrated coastal management in the mediterranean, including environmental aspects, water management, natural resource management, conservation and restoration of cultural heritage, socio-economic modernisation, etc. as regards developing countries, an indicative amount of eur 210 million is made available for specific activities such as those related to the uptake of innovation, the promotion of a sustainable relationship between population pressure, food security and the use and management of ecosystems, as well as productivity improvement and the prevention of the degradation of natural resources. in addition, a scholarship scheme has been set up under a specific programme which enables bachelor's degree holders and doctoral candidates from developing countries and mediterranean third countries to work on projects in the european framework. funds have been provided for coordination with community technical assistance programmes, including the meda and edf programmes, and for latin america and asia. it is, of course, the commission's responsibility to implement the three decisions mentioned above, drawing on the committees of these programmes. a call for proposals has been launched so far for developing countries and mediterranean countries, with a total budget of €85.5 million.

the rapporteur. - (fi) mr president, commissioner, this is an important amendment to the regulation which transfers powers from the council to the commission. this amendment to the regulation would enable the commission to amend the list of articles outside the first list. the present commission already has such rules for cereals, sugar, rice and eggs, for example. it would be consistent for the jurisdiction to extend to dairy products. the amendment to the regulation will transfer it to the commission. given that the financial envelope for export subsidies will be reduced in a few years from eur 587 million to eur 415 million as a result of the wto agreements, it would be good if the commission had the power to control the list of goods. there are many export goods which contain a large amount of non-dairy products - for example, yoghurt which contains sugar and fruit - and yet the export subsidy is used for dairy products. so it's very much about consistency so that we can do the same thing with grains, sugar, rice and eggs. it is also largely a question of budgetary discipline, so that in the years to come - when export credits have decreased - we can be bound accordingly to budgetary discipline. there has been some discussion that the transfer of competence from the council to the commission would not be a good thing. in my view, in this and similar situations, it is a game where there is no winner or loser. we have to be consistent and use the skills and take the steps that allow us to run the business in the best possible and logical way. this is not at all a question of altering the balance of power between the institutions. i think the arguments are reasonable and very consistent. i think that is the way we should proceed and move forward in the future - when prices and conditions change very rapidly on the world market - with practical things, so that we can react quickly. i think the committee on agriculture and rural development is unanimous on this point too. this decree must be changed. this is a good and positive thing, especially for all farmers and dairy farmers.

- (fi) mr president, the bilateral agreements between switzerland and the eu correspond to the multilateral agreements concluded within the framework of the european economic area. the group of the european liberal, democratic and reformist party believes that the european parliament should support its adoption. the swiss status is a concrete example of the strange nature of the current european integration system and the need to work to change it, especially now that the european union is about to expand and acquire pan-european dimensions. the unifying europe is arranged in concentric layers, the core of which is made up of the founding states of the european union. the first was the council of europe in strasbourg, which split in two when france, germany, italy and the benelux formed the european coal and steel community. the attempt to form a political and defence community failed, but the economic community was born. the seven countries outside the group of six formed the efta, the european free trade association. the enlargement of the six to a community of nine, then twelve, countries was relatively easy. after these enlargements, a debate has emerged on multi-speed integration. in the late 1980s, a project was drawn up within the commission, which envisaged that the ec of the day would become a federal state and that the other countries of europe would participate in the internal market through a system of the european economic area. the first eea agreement was proposed to the efta countries. that's when we first talked about concentric circles. the eea agreement was born, but the system was short-lived for austria, sweden and finland. in switzerland, membership of the eea was rejected in a referendum. as a result, only iceland, norway and liechtenstein remained in the eea. the process of establishing a system of bilateral agreements with switzerland was then started. negotiations are currently under way for the accession of 12 countries to the european union. it is strange that not a single eea country or switzerland is included in the number, whereas they are the ones who would have the best qualities to become members of the european union. there is a simple reason for this: they do not want to join the union that exists today or the one that is on the horizon. now that the european union is about to expand considerably, it is common sense for it to differentiate internally. just as jacques delors, valéry giscard d'estaing and helmut schmidt have proposed, the countries at the heart of the european union should go further than the others in their mutual integration, while the rest of the european union could evolve into a confederation of independent states and differentiate itself. this would facilitate and accelerate the accession of the eastern european states. the accession of iceland, norway, liechtenstein and even switzerland would then become possible.
the rapporteur. - (fi) mr president, like many other former soviet republics which have become independent, moldova is carrying the heavy burden of communism. both human and economically, moldova is doing very badly. the social situation is confused and the process towards democracy is painfully slow. the civil war and the fallout from the economic crisis paralysed russia for a long time. industry remains underdeveloped and unevenly distributed across regions. the small exports are based on the sale of agricultural products to neighbouring regions. the devastation is also reflected in the numbers. since 1992, the moldovan economy has shrunk by almost 70%, life expectancy has fallen sharply and, according to un studies last year, 50% of the population lives below the absolute poverty line. the commission is to be commended for proposing, on the initiative of the world bank and the international monetary fund, to provide moldova with substantial additional financial assistance. bilateral financial assistance through the tacis programme has produced relatively satisfactory results. in particular, significant progress has been made in training local government, eradicating corruption and promoting exports and agriculture. but that is not enough. the provision of financial assistance must be increased where, viewed from a macroeconomic point of view, it will make it possible to act on the substance of the problem: the strengthening of democratic development and economic and financial policy. the positive development that is taking place in moldova must be supported by measures that strengthen the democratic system above all. the commission must ensure that the additional financial support is used appropriately in this respect: flexibly and by strengthening the basic structures of society. as the biggest problem of the moldovan economy is its dependence on russia, the new eu support measures must aim precisely at ending this dependence. for example, moldova is entirely dependent on imported energy from russia and is thus under the thumb of russian energy companies. this spiral can only be stopped by strengthening moldova's energy sector and diversifying the country's exports. the other problem related to russia is the withdrawal of russian troops from moldovan territory. the european union must do everything it can to make the duma understand that the withdrawal of russian troops from moldova would help stabilise the country. the negotiations on the destruction of russian munitions stored in moldova should also be started without delay. my report was unanimously adopted in committee. the committee on budgets has added useful and justified points to it. i hope that the same wind of unanimity will also blow in the plenary tomorrow.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. - (fi) mr president, as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on budgets, i would like to draw attention to the economic impact of this loan decision. the european union provides various forms of economic aid to different countries. macroeconomic assistance is one of the most important; similar support may also be provided in the form of european investment bank guarantees and euratom loans. however, these loans have a ceiling which has been fixed in interinstitutional agreements. this is the annual ceiling of €200 million for the guarantee fund. this is a problem for any new loans that the european union might be planning to grant, because this ceiling is fixed. the loan and guarantee decisions that we are aware of may lead to the ceiling being exceeded if something unforeseen happens. i therefore think that it would be important for the committee on budgets to see the commission develop a multiannual indicative assessment of the likely future funding needs. the aim would be for parliament and the council to agree on priorities for the use of the remaining margin and for a procedure to be established for the amendments to be made to these principles. in the case of macroeconomic aid, the commission seems to have great confidence in the ability of the international institutions, the international monetary fund and the world bank, to assess the economic situation of the countries concerned. the role of these institutions is perhaps a little too important and the commission often automatically adopts the conditions imposed by others on these loans. that is why i consider the remarks and amendments by the rapporteur, mrs matikainen-kallström, very important, since they take into account the fact that the definition of the use of loans should also take account of employment and social security.

interfere with each other's budgets. however, if clear operational appropriations, such as the financing of the activities of the special envoys, are transferred to the council budget, this is no longer a clear administrative expenditure of the council but a common financing of the activities of the european union. the european parliament must therefore have the right to express its opinion because salaries represent the largest expenditure. we are currently experiencing a major phase of change at the level of administration as we strive to move to management by budget headings, to and the need to improve parliament's administration. it is therefore somewhat surprising, mr president, that important decisions on the development of the administration are already being taken before the study in question has even begun. a huge increase of 12.2 per cent in staff appropriations is in the budget. this increase is so significant that no other institution could even imagine such an increase in administrative expenditure. the increase now means the creation of 33 new posts and, apparently on the proposal of the chairmen, 12 more will be created. for the record, the european parliament created a total of only 11 new posts between 1995 and 1999. it is interesting to note that the proposal even includes the creation of a new post in the directorate-general for finance and financial control, whereas the question is whether prior control should be abandoned altogether. in addition, the budget contains a considerable number of new promotions. i cannot therefore agree with those who claim that the budget respects strict budgetary discipline: on the contrary, it is a budget of a rare laxity and it is difficult in this case to obtain the approval of the citizens for the budget of the european parliament. mr president, the time has come to react to the policy of the european parliament as an employer. the reform of the administration, the management by posts and the increase of the result requirement to the bottom of the scale require negotiations with the staff. the resources must be re-targeted and, as a result, the staffing regulations must be reformed. it would therefore have been appropriate to reserve the negotiating possibilities and instruments to advance these reforms instead of granting new advantages before having even started negotiations with staff on the corresponding administrative reforms.

commission - (de) mr president, the screening - the legal alignment - has of course been completely closed with all the candidates for a very long time. it is updated occasionally because both the european parliament, the council and the commission do not remain inactive and continue to produce legal acts, which change the acquis. it must of course be included in the negotiations. nevertheless, it is a regular occurrence. so, the screening process is closed with all the candidates. but there's a misunderstanding underlying your question. screening is not negotiation, it's just alignment. this screening is aimed only at establishing what the eu's acquis communautaire is, what is already part of the legislation of the candidate countries and what is not. this phase is over. you want to know where the negotiations are going as a whole. i can tell you in this connection that for the luxembourg group of states, with which negotiations were opened in march 1998, 10 to 15 of the 31 negotiating chapters have been closed for the time being. a few more chapters will be closed during the portuguese presidency. but the number of open chapters may be more important than the number of closed chapters. in this connection, by the end of the portuguese presidency, the 29 chapters concerning the luxembourg group states will be on the negotiating table. only the chapters 'institutions' and 'other' remain closed. i see. we'll deal with it at the end. for the helsinki group of states, negotiations only started in february this year. between 5 and 8 chapters will be opened with these countries during the portuguese presidency. i think we may even be able to temporarily close a number of these chapters at the end of the portuguese presidency, but at the moment it is difficult to is certainly as large as that of all the countries of the european union combined. mr savary proposed in the amendment concerning article 5 (5) that special attention be paid to material from third countries when examining specific cases. the proposal is good, but for finland it is only half the solution. it would result in a needless and laborious examination of russian railway cars and lead to a gigantic bureaucracy. it is also not certain that russian wagons will be considered as special cases in all cases. we have therefore tabled an amendment concerning article 7, which simply aims to allow wagons from a third country to be used in railway traffic where they do not cross the border between two member states. this is an addition to the commission's proposal, a point f. the adoption of this proposal would ensure that rail traffic in finland can continue without a catastrophic reduction.

the rapporteur. - (fi) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the liberalization of the telecommunications sector has been going on for a decade and has been a success. there's not much left of the traditional telephone. in the past, people had the telephone to keep in touch with each other, today they have become shareholders in companies that transmit pink services, for example. this has certainly surprised many. the device itself is so small that it can be accidentally placed in the mouth in place of the tooth. again, he has changed a lot. liberalisation has raised fears about the weakening of public services and the increase in the price of communications. none of this has happened: services have diversified and prices have fallen. new terminal designs, networks and services have of course brought in money. the 1998 regulation was timely and has worked well in many member states. it is true that in some member states implementation has been slow and even bureaucratic. we also share the commission's view that crossborder services and competition could be improved. we are concerned that the limited supply at the local loop level has prevented many users, especially those with fewer resources, from taking advantage of affordable internet connections, for example. alternative forms of access are needed, wireless forms of access must be developed, and cable television networks, for example, must be operated without recourse to specific legislation. the european parliament notes that the current model of action has, due to its changing nature, led to legal uncertainty in different member states. the specific conditions of licences must be sufficiently transparent to prevent unnecessary delays in the decision to grant them. the most important reform strategy of the european union is the conversion programme known as 'eeurope', the framework for which has just been adopted in lisbon. the commission's contribution to the european information society structure and strategy is positive in that it takes account not only of competitiveness but also of, for example, cohesion, social equality and employment. the aim is to prevent social exclusion and to strengthen citizens' confidence and social cohesion. the title of the whole programme is eloquent: the information society for all. radio frequencies are a limited natural resource, which has been put up for auction. our position in this respect is negative, except in a few specific cases. we knew two years ago that the auctioning of the gsm network in the united states had caused chaos and bankruptcies. india has a bad record in this area too. according to the auction's proponents, this is a good way of managing resources. in our view, this leads to higher subscriber charges and slower expansion and development of communication services. the auction prices have reached an incredibly high level and will be reflected in the prices paid by users, which is equivalent to a new tax on telecommunications, an unfair tax in that it affects equally the rich and the poor. now that we have adopted the eeurope programme with all its laudable objectives, it is being put on a brake, a new 'digital divide', by a greedy principle of charging the maximum. in practice, our pilot project, the development of the information society, is being held back by a tax. this is a matter of concern which i think should be the subject of our investigations and i ask the commission to study without delay the effects in the different countries, in other words to see what impacts the auctioning principle has had, so that we can take an informed position. in addition, this principle favours large players, who are beginning to share the market, and it is feared that we will end up in the following situation: while we 'deregulate', they will understand each other and 're-regulate', which cannot be acceptable.

the rapporteur. - (sv) mr president, commissioner, when it comes to this report, it seems that i am being prosecuted under the law of maximum bad luck, since i was myself in romania when the committee on industry, foreign trade, research and energy was voted. i would like to thank mr van welzen for his efforts to open the debate in my absence. i think it is important to remember what the situation was when the directive was adopted. it was then thought that conditional access was the major obstacle to opening up television services. today, the situation may be different: if we are not careful, other channels of television distribution will be bottlenecks or obstacles. it is necessary to require that different computer programmes can communicate with each other, and not merely that technical equipment must be used simultaneously. at the same time, consumers have a great need for easily accessible information and reasonable requirements to anticipate market developments. we will be facing a future situation where digital television, i think, will be part of the increasingly integrated electronic functions of home use. it's important that we have an api, an application programming interface, that can be used in this set. this is a key point, including for the use of electronic programme guides. these two factors will have to determine the direction of future provisions. i would also like to draw the attention of my colleagues to the importance of these electronic programme guides, for example, when it comes to parents protecting their minor children from unwanted broadcasts. neither the operating systems of the decoders nor the electronic programming values are compatible with each other at present. the committee, like the commission, believes that the markets and technologies in the field of digital television have reached a stage of development which is beyond the scope of the directive. the purpose of the regulation established by the directive we are discussing today should therefore be revised. it would be good if this question were to be included in the package of amendments to the directive on the communication sector which we expect from the commission in a few weeks' time. the rapporteur's conclusion is that we need open standards today. we are now in the second and third generation of digital television equipment. work towards the creation of such open standards for home multimedia equipment is well under way and it is hoped that the european telecommunications standards institute, etsi, will adopt the mhp standard. there is good reason to believe that this standard could provide a valid basis for future developments. however, it cannot be excluded that this choice of standards will be the subject of political decisions in the future if the market fails to agree on its own. digital television services are distributed in several different ways, not all of which are technically equivalent. like traditional television, digital television can be broadcast via terrestrial networks, cable, and satellite. these distribution systems now have a variable impact, but the satellite is dominant. however, it is possible that cable distribution will gain ground in the future. cable distribution provides an integrated feedback channel that provides direct access to the interactive services offered on the network. i am convinced that access to these interactive services is the alpha and omega of the way forward if we want digital television to become one of the gateways to the information society. these different distribution systems also mean that digital television can become a key key to internet access in areas of low population density. the end of analogue television broadcasting and the total switch to digital television are important conditions for the efficient exploitation of the limited resources of the radio spectrum. no one has so far dared to define the conditions under which the end of analogue broadcasting could come. digitisation poses challenges, perhaps especially for public service companies. these companies cannot finance the additional equipment needed as conventional operators can. how will the recovery of licence fees be ensured in countries which finance television activity in this way and where the principle of free-to-air tradition is applied? i would also like to stress that the rapporteur supports the principle of 'must carry', that is to say the obligation for cable companies to provide and maintain channels. but we cannot make demands that will precisely hinder the development of interactive services or jeopardize the willingness to invest in improving the network. even public service broadcasters that once broadcast their programs freely now impose limits and create obstacles to international broadcasts. in the background are the copyright issues. the organisations responsible for managing the copyright of television companies should make use of the new possibilities to enable those who wish to watch digital programmes to do so even if they are outside the borders of the broadcasting country. this is an objective which the treaty on european union already requires us to achieve, in accordance with the 'television without frontiers' directive.

than the lowest rate. the practice of excise duties is not an act of fiscal policy aimed solely at raising funds for the state; it is clearly an instrument which promotes a healthier way of life through the means of the market economy.

- (fi) mr president, the decisions taken at the helsinki european council with a view to developing a common security and defence policy give rise to interpretations. several allied member states consider that the formation of a common military force is the green light for the organisation of a common defence of the union. their plan is clear. in accordance with the basic treaties, the objective of the common foreign and defence policy is to guarantee the independence and inviolability of the union and to strengthen the security of the union in all its forms. from their point of view, the military corps to be formed is an instrument to achieve these objectives, thus a common army of the union on the way to becoming a federal state. according to the neutral member states, the decision was only taken to set up a crisis management system. they stress that each country will decide separately on its participation. they consider that they are not obliged to participate in the peace obligation. the neutral member states do not want the union to become a military alliance and a great power. these differences in view and interpretation will have to be taken into account. it is also wise to work towards differentiated integration in security policy. the european union's joint bodies should be mobilised only for crisis management missions. the countries forming part of the federal nucleus proposed by mr delors, mr giscard d'estaing, mr schmidt and mr fischer could create a defence community which would form a strong european pillar within nato. the understandable and worthy effort of the european nato member countries to promote a european defensive identity would therefore be carried out within the framework of nato. decisions on security and defence policy are extremely important. that is why we must take them democratically, with more openness.

in writing. - (fi) in her report, mrs lulling strongly criticised excise duties on alcohol in finland, which she considered to be excessively high. it also criticised efforts to restrict the import of beer through derogations. in my view, the commission's proposal is a fairly good compromise. it argued that finland would retain the right to gradually lift the more stringent restrictions on imports from other eu countries by the end of 2003. the import of beer from third countries such as russia and estonia could be limited to six litres until 2006. nowhere else on the eu's external borders is alcohol as cheap as in the regions bordering finland. in my view, finland therefore has good reasons for maintaining stricter import restrictions than other community countries. as regards internal market policy, public health and environmental protection aspects must also be taken into account, in accordance with the treaty of amsterdam. derogations from community legislation should be allowed if they are justified by the need to improve the protection of public health.

the rapporteur. - (fi) mr president, the draft supplementary and amending budget 2/2000 under consideration contains appropriations for the council and parliament. the council's budget is eur 6.635 million. this amount is earmarked for the common foreign and security policy and for defence policy. the cost of renting, renovating and furnishing a new building is estimated at €5.877 million. this building will be used for the needs of the staff of the foreign and security policy. the european union's general staff will be based there, along with military experts from the member states. the number of participants should be 45 in the initial phase but should eventually reach 60 or even 90 people. the salary costs of these staff will be eur 758 000 at the end of the year. in accordance with an old 'gentleman's agreement', no institution - neither the council nor parliament - interfered with each other's draft budgets. as regards the european parliament, the draft supplementary and amending budget contains an appropriation of eur 15 million for an additional instalment for the brussels building. this will reduce annual interest costs by eur 2.2 million for ten years. the margin for the financial perspective will be eur 69.1 million after these appropriations. the committee on budgets has already transferred eur 38 million to finance this building, the spinelli, by means of a c6/2000 transfer of funds not used this year. since the president was able to make transfers for this purpose, some eur 58 million will be available for accelerated payments. this will reduce parliament's annual payments by some eur 8-9 million over a period of 10 years, which is a great relief. it is reasonable for the european parliament to pay for these premises in advance. this will save interest and give parliament room for manoeuvre in its budget for the years ahead. this flexibility will be necessary when parliament's other expenditure increases with eastward enlargement. in accordance with the supplementary and amending budget 2/2000, parliament's share of expenditure under heading 5, administrative expenditure, is already 20.41%, which is just above the 20% ceiling set by parliament itself. at first, the share of administrative expenditure was less than 20% when the budget was drawn up. since it is very likely that a large part of the appropriation - eur 60 million - allocated to the members' regulation will not be used, since more than half the year has already passed, this ceiling can be reviewed towards the end of the year. it should also be noted that parliament is saving money because the investment is extremely reasonable. the council's budget is interesting because it includes for the first time appropriations for the common security and defence policy. structures will be created in the council to link the defence dimension more closely to the eu as a sector of its action, in line with the helsinki summit resolutions. this will be extremely difficult for small neutral countries, such as finland. the devastation of the crisis management operation in kosovo is frightening. to what extent can war operations be justified and what are their limits? the future budgetary technique will pose a major problem. article 28 of the treaty is not clear, especially as regards the common security, defence and military policy. administrative expenditure is part of the eu budget but the council has the power of decision on operational expenditure. what will be the practical approach and what will be the share of this expenditure that will be allocated to heading 4 (external action), which is already overburdened? in future negotiations, the classification of expenditure will have to be clarified and brought more into line with parliament's wishes.

the commission is aware of the seriousness of the problem of pollution of industrial sites in europe. according to the european environment agency's report on the state of the environment in the eu, the 1998 centre for land issues estimated the number of sites that are polluted or likely to be polluted at 750,000. the current european legislation, as well as the one currently being drafted, focuses on pollution prevention. directive 96/61 on the harmonisation of measures to prevent and control pollution, the ippc directive, establishes the obligation for member states to take the necessary measures to clean up abandoned industrial sites. such measures must be implemented at the stage of issuing operating authorisations. this directive will have to be fully applied from november 2007. the directive we are currently drawing up on environmental responsibility is another instrument which should help to prevent land pollution in the future. as regards old pollution, some member states have developed special financial instruments - for such cases there are taxes or special funds. it is already clear that this type of remedial work is costing or will cost member states very large sums of money. at european level, the regional development fund can provide some help. i can also say that i am aware of the importance of the problem for the applicant countries. however, the commission does not intend to launch a comprehensive strategy for sanitation at european level. as regards long-term land pollution, the principle of subsidiarity should be applied, as national, regional or local authorities are better placed to deal with such issues. this is more than one kind of pollution that does not spread across borders. moreover, the problem is primarily financial, not administrative.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat pollution of industrial sites. as i said, we know that this issue is also a serious economic concern for many candidate countries, which frequently mention the enormous scale of the problem. so far we have mainly looked at this from a local and national perspective. in order to be able to clean up the sites concerned in the best possible way, it is necessary to know what type of pollution is involved and what the local context is. we have nothing against the idea of setting up a common strategy, if the member states also wish to do so, but then we will need the means. so far it seems that this issue has not been considered a real priority, but i think it is of course very important that there should be a real inventory of sites, that the member states themselves should launch a programme to this end, and why not also research and development efforts in this area, in order to find the most efficient methods for the remediation of industrial sites.
