the rapporteur. - (sv) mr president, madam commissioner and mr european ombudsman, the opinion of the rapporteurs and the committee on the european ombudsman's report for 1999 - a year marked by crises within the european institutions and by a deep distrust of the administration - is that citizens will only find a real justification for european integration if they enjoy rights enabling them to participate actively in a political dialogue with the institutions. political dialogue is not a genuine dialogue, however, if citizens do not have the right to information and access to the documents they require, if the opinions they express are not taken into account, dealt with and taken seriously. what does this have to do with the european ombudsman's mission? the ombudsman's task, under the treaties, is to investigate cases of maladministration in the community institutions or bodies. at the request of the european parliament, the ombudsman gave a definition of the term 'maladministration' in the 1997 annual report. the law applies when a public body does not act in accordance with a rule or principle which it regards as having the force of law. the institutions agreed during the ombudsman's previous mandate to apply this definition, which now and in the future constitutes the starting point for the ombudsman's activities. the committee stresses in the report that this commonly agreed definition of 'maladministration' also implies the right to examine how the institutions interpret community law. whatever the legal form of the charter of fundamental rights, it will in any case be an expression of the constitutional traditions common to the member states, which, according to article 6 of the treaty, must be respected by the union and, consequently, by the institutions and bodies. the commission therefore considers that the european ombudsman should play an important role in the application of a future charter of fundamental rights of citizens. the commission has also clearly expressed its support for a charter of fundamental rights containing standards granting citizens the right to good administration within the eu. good administration is the opposite of bad administration. the right to good administration, as set out in the charter of citizens' rights and the code of good administrative behaviour promoted by parliament and the european ombudsman, includes: the right to be heard in his case, the right to consult documents, the right to a prompt response from the administration and to a reasoned response. all these aspects have been the focus of the ombudsman's work. these rights are all prerequisites for active citizenship, which is necessary if the eu is to regain the trust of its citizens. it is these rights that make citizenship active rather than passive. good administration can create the conditions for active citizenship, while bad administration can undermine the conditions for it. what are the most common types of complaints addressed to the european ombudsman? incomplete information or refusal of information, avoidable delay, discrimination, lack of right to a defence. people want good government. they want to be active citizens. these conclusions can be drawn from the ever-increasing number of complaints to the ombudsman and from what they entail. the ombudsman has become known for his work in favour of greater publicity. for this he deserves great praise. a survey he carried out on his own initiative several years ago has now enabled most community institutions and bodies to have regulations on public access to documents. he continued his tireless work for genuine transparency and for data confidentiality and the protection of individuals not to be used to impede the transparency of the activities of the authorities vis-à-vis the public. gradually, the european ombudsman has developed his work, worked for better administration and won the trust of citizens. its cooperation with national ombudsmen and, subsequently, with similar bodies in the candidate countries is equally important. this is serious work which parliament is right to support. this support must be provided by providing the ombudsman with sufficient financial resources to enable him to carry out the demanding inquiries entailed by the mandate. we must also be careful now to support a mediator who is independent in his decisions. i would like to thank the ombudsman and his office and the secretariat of the committee on petitions, which, as we know today, is working in difficult conditions.
- (fi) mr president, madam commissioner, mr ombudsman, it may be useful to recall that only a year and a half ago, there was a discussion in this building about the need for a committee on petitions. i am very pleased to see that the committee on petitions has proved its usefulness, especially at a time when we want to strengthen the position of the citizen of the european union. i really hope that the committee on petitions will succeed in cooperating effectively with the other committees over the coming year. i am convinced that we will be able to draw up joint reports on the main problem areas. as for the mediator, he has indeed established his authority and independence. the growing number of complaints is proof that citizens have "found it". we members of the european parliament have often been able to guide citizens to the ombudsman so that they can find a solution to their problems. in future, easy access to documents will be an issue that will give us work and in this area we have already received valuable advice from the ombudsman.
- (fi) mr president, mr ombudsman, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i thank the rapporteurs for their very thorough and serious work. the mediator monitors and remedies the conflict between power and the object of power. there were almost a few thousand cases last year. i propose seven principles. the larger the union, the greater the need for transparency. the younger it is, the greater the need, and the more complicated it is, the better the transparency. the more we move towards an information society, the more open it must be and the more civilized it is, the more public it is. the more multicultural it is, the more transparent it is, and the more democratic it is, the more open it is. we're used to all appliances being used, even kitchen whips. the principles of good administration, created with merit by the ombudsman, constitute the european bureaucracy's way of working for the citizens. and it's true that this manual is useful! there is no democracy without good administration and for that, the principles of good administration must be included in the union's founding act. another important point: in the information society, the transparency of files and the principles governing them must be brought to a level worthy of it by means of new technologies. in other words, you need to be able to file a complaint online. prior decisions and related documents must also be available in real time. my proposal is that we need a portal from the ombudsman to the internet. thank you for your work !

in writing. - (fi) the harmfulness of phthalates to human health has not been scientifically established. for example, according to research conducted in the united states by the u.s. consumer product safety commission, very few, if any, children may be at risk of exposure to hazardous chemicals because the phthalates in toys are not at levels that could be dangerous. since the quantities of phthalates that could possibly pass from toys into a child's body are so small, the study group does not recommend banning phthalates. i myself am not in favour of excessive precautions or of a too strict prohibition which is not scientifically based. the sudden toxic effects of phthalates are minimal and do not cause mucosal or skin irritation. although it was found in the 1980s that very large quantities of phthalates caused liver tumours in rats and mice, official sources in europe and the united states have not confirmed the carcinogenic nature of phthalates in humans. i also do not like the idea of replacing phthalates with substances for which we have no scientific studies. i am, however, in favour of further studies on phthalates, the effects of which have not been confirmed. in practice, the problem is reduced in europe because the european toy industry has largely given up phthalates.
- (fi) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, short sea shipping is the most economical, efficient and appropriate mode of transport for remote regions. i would like to thank mrs peijs for her interesting presentation. in my country, shipping is almost entirely short sea shipping. so it's important to us. we are also an exception because our only land link with the rest of the eu is in the north and so the importance of maritime transport is considerable. finally, the distance of our railways differs from that of the rest of the european union. the most peculiar thing is that in my country shipping always stops in winter if we do not take measures because the sea freezes. for this reason, we had to create a special fleet to break the ice. they are strong ships, with power equal to that used by a city. so, since we have this "disability to travel", we want to subsidize this icebreaking because we can't help it, it's an obstacle wanted by the creator. it is also regrettable that the social and employment impact of this trafficking is not sufficiently highlighted. cheap labour is a huge risk in these conditions, so especially in the north when the sea is frozen. how can a person, who normally wears shorts, who has almost no professional skills, manage to cope with thirty degrees below zero and three metres of ice? that's the problem. the airline staff are always well trained but the same cannot be said of the cheap labour that works on the ships. i therefore expect the commission to intervene in this matter.

- mr president, i would like to emphasize the objective of using efficient alternative means of transport instead of short flights, which is the objective of this report. for example, high-speed trains, and in some cases short sea shipping, offer a more environmentally friendly alternative to short flights. this is necessary both for congestion and environmental reasons. the replacement of short flights by alternative modes of transport will make room for long flights for which there is no alternative. before the summer break, parliament examined a number of reports aimed at improving the competitiveness of rail and maritime transport. in addition to improving the competitiveness of rail and maritime transport, other measures are needed. one possibility is the environmental levy planned for air transport. it must be introduced in such a way that it imposes less burden on unnecessary long flights than on short flights for which a more environmentally friendly alternative is available.

- mr president, the importance of sport as an economic, social, health and cultural force is undeniable. it is incomprehensible that sport lacks a legal basis in the european union. the european parliament must, quite frankly, force the commission and the council to look at themselves in the mirror. the status of sport must be defined in a clear way at the next intergovernmental conference, so that sports organisations can participate in decision-making on sport. as the sums involved in top-level sport are enormous, the general rules of the game must be drawn up taking into account the interests of the system's pillars, the athletes and the organisers of sporting events. the interests of team sportsmen must be safeguarded, respecting the principle of free movement of labour and not forgetting the financial compensation due to the so-called breeding associations. in the case of the television coverage of major sporting events, a balance must be struck between the financial power of the organisers and the so-called universal service principle. when we talk about doping, as in any other sporting discussion, we must make a clear distinction between fitness and elite sport. the ethical problems of elite sports are well known. the widespread use of doping by ordinary sportsmen, as evidenced by the trade in banned substances that is boasted about in gyms, has received too little attention. the problem is comparable to the problem of drugs, especially the use of synthetic drugs, and for reasons of public health alone, the eu must also play its part in tackling the problem.
- mr president, our group discussed this morning how we would react to paragraph 5 of the motion for a resolution on cloning, which states that there is no distinction between reproductive and other cloning. the matter is controversial and depends strongly on the viewpoint chosen. there are two points of view that are really indistinguishable. firstly, technically, in the implementation, it is the same event. secondly, philosophically, if the measure is viewed from the perspective of the foetus, there is no difference. in any case, it is used as a tool, as a commodity, not as a being with intrinsic value. there is a moving consensus that cloning for reproductive purposes is unethical. my deep concern is with the grounds we use to oppose it. and i'm doing this as a person with a philosophy degree. are our grounds such that they can be waived later on on the grounds that we are, in fact, already technically cloning? does it matter what the intent is when the act is the same? i voted against amendment no 5 today only because i think it lacks justification. i do not think, however, that the separation is justified from the point of view of the technical means, or even of the foetus. so such a separation should not be accepted without a really thorough discussion.
- mr president, in finland the common people say that gentlemen's business is slow. this is also the case in the context of the 1996 political accounts for the year of lifelong learning. despite the time wasted, the rapporteur regrets that the qualitative assessment of the implementation of the programme for the year is lacking. of course, you can calculate the number of opportunities in a shorter time. at the european paper industry seminar here in strasbourg on wednesday, a representative of the paper industry drew attention to an important issue. when you buy hardware, it's always an investment, but when you buy software called training for the company's staff, it's not considered an investment. the rapporteur has rightly emphasized this point. training must be seen as an investment and not as a current expense. it should be emphasised that lifelong learning includes a general education component and not just a vocational component. all too often, education is too one-sided, and this is particularly true today with the development of the information society. electronic programs are complex and are becoming more complex all the time. those who know the language of software often do not understand the language of content. we can ask, as we do in finland: a man speaks six languages, but what does he have to say? in the information society, someone can speak machine language, but they have nothing else to say. there's only zeroes and ones. this is where we come across the eeurope strategy that the commission prepared for the lisbon summit. it's paid a lot of attention to the hardware, but little attention to the software, which is called education. citizens must be trained not only to use machines but also to use machines for their own benefit. machines must be used for useful and necessary purposes which improve man's ability to function and control his own life. today, every person must be literate to take advantage of the various forms of electronic media. this new reading skill must be taught to many adults from the beginning of the alphabet. the eeurope programme's goal of bringing computer cables to every home is a democratic step forward. in the modern world, democracy is achieved by giving everyone equal access to a wide range of information. to put knowledge to good use, it is important to remember that learning is also a social event. the eeurope programme cannot be left to the training alone, but it also needs the social partners. since no one can know everything, a person must be able to decide for himself what is worth knowing. if it is decided by the state, it is easy to go over to the side of propaganda and indoctrination. when we look at eu programmes, which are often the same for everyone or which require harmonization for everyone, we are turning the eu into a totalitarian federation. we need lifelong learning and general education to be able to resist the european union's attempt to create a totalitarian state in europe and to be its politburo. i 'm not .

approach both in the short and long term. the individual measures taken by individual countries under the pressure of protests only aggravate the situation and distort the conditions of competition. in the structure of the refining chain, the nature of the oil energy market, the futures method and the storage policy of certain countries or regions. it has been argued that the chain of processing mr president, we should not be surprised at all that we are now again in the midst of an oil crisis. this is the third oil crisis and since 1979 there has been nothing but negligence in energy policy. much has been said about the transition to a sustainable energy policy, but very little has been done. i welcome the ideas that the commissioner has put forward here today. transport ministers now have a real opportunity to make a direct u-turn in energy policy towards a sustainable energy economy. road transport has increased from 50% in 1980 to 85% in 2000. if we now reduce the fuel tax in general, this would be about the same as reducing the price of heroin on the drug market. the result would probably be more drug addicts and a deeper crisis. this is not the kind of mistake you should be making. nuclear power is also not an option in this situation, because only 10% of electricity is produced by oil and, moreover, all development work on new vehicles, for example, is based on the fact that the cars of the future will run on hydrogen, but not necessarily on nuclear power. our group has today presented such a comprehensive programme as the new energy policy programme of the european union. we want to start implementing this from the budget, we are presenting a new budget line called 'new energy sources'. we want to promote energy saving and better building techniques in buildings, among other things. we also want the european union to have the powers to manage the new energy policy. we demand that energy be included in the treaty as a chapter in its own right and we stress the importance of abandoning the unanimity requirement in relation to taxes. for nearly ten years, there have been attempts to create a common energy tax for the european union. the commissioners have come and gone, but this problem has not been solved. in the short term, we must help the immediate victims of this oil crisis, but we must do so in a very deliberate and targeted way.
mr president, i would like to thank mr hernández mollar for the valuable work he has done in drawing up this report. in the field of data protection, a difficult trade-off must be made between the protection of privacy, the security of citizens and the sound functioning of the internal market. the basic requirement for a market in a frontier-free europe is that information should be able to move freely and quickly. with the disappearance of visible controls on the movement of goods and services, the importance of so-called invisible controls has increased year by year. the rapid exchange of information between authorities is the only way to prevent criminals from taking advantage of free movement. the level of data protection in the current bitstream is not yet adequate in the european union. the fundamental human right to postal security is under threat, inter alia, through surveillance and criminal activity in e-mail systems. the rapid growth of electronic commerce is creating new threats to the protection of citizens' data. for example, the theft of credit card information or the misuse of personal identification are becoming more common in computer networks. furthermore, databases created for marketing purposes containing detailed information on people's lifestyles and consumption habits are often in conflict with fundamental rights protecting privacy. it is reasonable to assume that the work of the various data protection authorities now operating in the eu could be made more efficient by means of centralisation. the establishment of a common authority is a great thing in terms of the functioning of europol, customs supervision and the fundamental rights of ordinary eu citizens. at the same time, however, it is necessary to ensure the adequacy of operational resources. the workload is growing at a rapid pace. it is therefore necessary to find out how to improve cooperation on data protection between the newly created secretariat and the national authorities. the independence of data protection authorities is at the heart of a successful operation. control is always a matter of difficult interpretations, which many operators, motivated by purely economic interests, wish to influence. this is why the authorities should work in close cooperation with the eu and national courts. the european parliament must closely monitor the activities and accountability of the newly created official secretariat, precisely from the point of view of impartiality.
mr president, i would like to congratulate mr ferrer on his balanced report. it is important that we recognise the role and the potential of the eu; we call for coherence and coordination. i would also like to express my satisfaction at the fact that the work of ngos is recognised and taken into account in development cooperation, precisely in order to strengthen civil society through this cooperation. i also think it is a healthy starting point to recommend that coordination be carried out within the framework of the strategies chosen by the countries themselves. when we talk about development cooperation, it is also a healthy reminder that development cooperation is not an end in itself. the aim is to enable developing countries to manage their own affairs, to take responsibility for their own development and to act as just societies; and in so far as we ourselves make these opportunities difficult, we will try to remedy the situation. at eu level, one of the important things is that our policy does not encourage people to live off one product. only countries with healthy economies can benefit from globalization. the developing countries that are stuck with one product are in a difficult situation. at eu level, we must also ensure that values are reflected in our development cooperation policy. there has to be a place for conditioning. we must not support countries which do not show any interest in the human rights and democracy of their citizens. in other cases, it must be limited to humanitarian aid. i would also say that it is very necessary for the eu to have a human rights ombudsman, like the ombudsman, whose task would also be to monitor the human rights situation in developing countries. in fact, the risk is quite high. we must develop aid that is of a nature to prevent conflict, not to fuel it. aid must be targeted to the most deprived. the crises caused by environmental problems must be anticipated and aid must not be given in such a way as to intensify the struggle for natural resources. we must learn to identify those societies where the risk of violence is linked to state terrorism. the channelling of aid must take account of the fact that it does not undermine undemocratic power structures. conditionality can prevent oppression and corruption among certain groups. i know that it is not simple and that conditioning is laborious and difficult, but that much can be achieved with so-called positive conditioning. experience has shown that. that is why the eu should not be a stranger to it.
mr president, the commission is proposing changes to the trade regime for processed agricultural products. the aim is to end the aid and limit it by means of aid certificates. two fundamental developments are at play: on the one hand, the pressure to reduce export subsidies caused by the liberalisation of world trade, and on the other, the internal budgetary pressures within the eu, mainly related to the management of external relations. the food chain and ultimately the farmers will pay the bill. the market situation is already putting pressure on the european producer. in addition, costs are rising, due to, among other things, higher fuel costs and increased environmental and other obligations. the profitability of production is in flux and, to put it a little more bluntly, the question arises: does agricultural production make any sense in europe? the starting point of our committee and the group of the european liberal, democratic and reformist party is that the eu must safeguard the viability of the production chain. the commission proposes selective reductions. but we are in a bind. i think that is the way to go, but there are certain principles that must be followed. the amendments must take account of the small companies in the sector. for example, in my country there simply cannot be large export companies because of the small population. there should be flexibility in setting the limits of the export refund procedure. the form of amendment no 8 is therefore justified. it gives flexibility specifically to small businesses. i would also like to point out that the effects of replacing export cuts with inward processing and additional procedures are uneven in that transport costs are a heavy burden for the more remote eu countries, and i therefore agree with the rapporteur that such procedures cannot be used for the long term. it is precisely the interim solutions that are at issue. our group supports the amendments tabled by the committee.

mr president, commissioner vitorino, it is important for the electronic commerce market that trade can be conducted in an electronic environment on a pan-european basis. there should be no limits to the market, either by legislation or technology. a europe without frontiers is in the interests of consumers. if the amendments to the report now before us in committee were rejected tomorrow, the future of electronic commerce in europe would be in jeopardy. as pleasant as it would be for the employment situation of our legal profession, it would be a huge burden for small and mediumsized enterprises in europe to take account of the legislation of all eu countries and of the countries that have signed the lucano agreement when conducting electronic commerce. this would probably also mean that those decent companies who want to take responsibility and care for their consumers would not be involved in e-commerce at all. as a result, the total supply to consumers is reduced, as lord inglewood has already mentioned. on the other hand, the irresponsible 'screw-me-ups' who take care of the consumer only until the payment has been credited to the seller's account would remain on the market. the consumer organisation proposed that marketing could only be extended to one country or to certain markets, which is not even technically possible in the internet world. the electronic world knows no national boundaries. moreover, this would be discriminatory towards consumers, who should have the right to order products from anywhere. such an interpretation would also be likely to be contrary to the free movement of goods and services within the meaning of article 49 of the treaty of rome. the e-commerce company usually cannot even know which country is the consumer's home country. it would therefore be impossible to require that the company first ascertain the consumer protection legislation in the customer's country of origin, since any country can be the home country. it is reasonable to develop alternative dispute resolution procedures instead. however, parties to electronic commerce should have the right to choose between ordinary judicial proceedings and alternative dispute resolution procedures. i would also ask the council and the commission to consider, in the course of their work, whether it would be sensible to consider this chargeback mechanism, which mr de palacio, in his amendment, has re-introduced in this report. it can add bureaucracy for the consumer. the committee's position on this is not perfect either, but it seems to be supported by a majority in this house and will remain parliament's position tomorrow, which is a good thing.

draftsman of the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs. – mr president, may i begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr skinner, for the excellent work he has done in drafting this report. as the draftsman of the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs and the shadow rapporteur for the epp-de group on this matter in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, i should like to express my sincere thanks to mr skinner who has been very successful in collating the views of parliamentary committees and political groups and putting them on paper in a harmonious and balanced manner. in the communication on risk capital, the main political message was that risk capital is essential for job-creation, raising productivity and supporting growth in europe. i would like to express this even more strongly than the commission did. if we really want the european union to achieve the ambitious goals of the lisbon summit, it is absolutely essential to improve the functioning of the single european risk capital market. companies in the european union, especially in growth industries like the hi-tech and bio-tech industries, cannot achieve their full potential without better access to risk capital. efficient venture capital markets are also a precondition for the development of entrepreneurship in europe. in that sense i fully agree with mr skinner when he says that it is of the utmost importance to give smes open and fair access to the new risk capital markets. according to a recent study mentioned in the commission communication, this is also a very efficient tool for improving employment, since between 80 and 90% of funds raised from venture capital normally go towards hiring more people; in other words more risk capital allows us to use our human capital more efficiently. in fighting unemployment, concrete measures to alleviate the tax pressure on labour are also urgently needed. i also want to emphasise the importance of reducing the costs and administrative procedures involved in setting up a company and getting back business after bankruptcy. at the moment there are too many possible entrepreneurs outside the economy, just because of inadequate insolvency rules. the present communication shows that even if some progress has been made europe still has some major weaknesses, particularly when compared with its main competitors. mr skinner said that both the amount and allocation of risk capital remains sub-optimal compared to the united states, and unfortunately this overall gap is widening all the time. that should make us a little worried about the timetable proposed by the heads of state and government in lisbon. we should ask ourselves whether the deadline of 2003 is too late for europe. i welcome the view put forward by gordon brown and others that we should think about accelerating the timetable, in both the risk capital action plan and the financial services action plan. we should at least make sure that some progress is made this year. i therefore totally support parliament's view on point 23, calling on the commission to keep us regularly informed about recent developments. the proposal for a directive on supplementary pension funds is essential for the growth of venture capital in europe. the employment and social committee wanted to make a reference to the findings of the recent report on supplementary pensions, particularly on the prudential rules. institutional investors such as insurance companies, investment funds and pension funds play an increasingly predominant role in providing risk capital. pension funds in particular have been gaining in importance in the last decade as the demographic changes increase the need for retirement products. the shift in favour of more performance-orientated instruments has also increased the need to reform the system. the developments over the last few days with the single currency have been unfortunate. the euro will never be strong or attractive enough if the european economy is not strong and attractive. the risk capital action plan is one of the political choices we have already decided to make. now we only have to make sure that we make it fast enough to use the current window of opportunity.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, on behalf of my group i would like to thank the rapporteur for the thorough work he has done on this important subject. the purpose of the transeuropean networks is precisely to link the different regions of the eu together so that the common market can function effectively. the rapporteur has, however, looked at the matter mainly from the point of view of the major ports. the tonnage of cargo is an important indicator, but it does not always tell the whole story. the quality of the cargo also plays a decisive role in the tonnage. the ports in the peripheral regions are particularly important, although the freight and passenger volumes are lower than in the core areas. the proposal to increase the tonnage of cargo to 1.5 million tonnes for seaports and to change the classification of ports does not, in the view of our group, serve the development of transeuropean networks. this would mean that ports in the ten would be concentrated in densely populated core areas and many sparsely populated peripheral areas would be excluded from the ten. the second disadvantage is that only international traffic is counted in the passenger numbers of the ten ports. for example, ferry traffic between scotland and northern ireland carries 1.8 million passengers a year, but these ports would be excluded from the ten if only international traffic were included. this link is not only important between scotland and northern ireland but also for the republic of ireland. the smaller, important ports and also the intra-union passenger traffic must be taken into account more closely, which is why our group opposes amendments nos 3, 4, 5, 9 and 10, amongst others. in this respect, the council's position is more conducive to the development of transeuropean networks.
- mr president, mr prodi, as has been said many times in this chamber, the charter of fundamental rights is the result of genuine democratic cooperation. it was worked on by representatives of our own parliament and of national parliaments and governments. this cooperation, despite the doubts, produced an excellent result. may i ask whether this successful model of cooperation should be continued in the future? many citizens feel that the european union is remote and bureaucratic. the citizens' distrust of the union must be taken seriously. we must pay particular attention to the relationship between citizens and the institutions of the union. i am therefore very pleased that the charter of fundamental rights emphasises the principle of transparency, although it could be even more emphatic. despite the demands made in this house today, it seems that the charter is becoming not a legally binding document but a political declaration. however, the expression 'only' which i have used may be called into question, because i believe that, despite its declaratory nature, the court of justice will apply the articles of the charter in its case-law as self-binding principles, with which the court's interpretation must naturally be in harmony. in this way, they are gradually becoming de facto legally binding legal provisions. the charter of fundamental rights is a sign of the union's responsibility and concern for human rights. however, in order to monitor the implementation of human rights in practice, the union must have clear rules on possible sanction mechanisms against member states. the council is currently considering the inclusion of such mechanisms in article 7. the agreement on procedures is a good thing in itself, but care must be taken that the system cannot be used as a political weapon. there's an old finnish saying, "you have to study things first, then you have to waste them". at the moment, i am afraid that if the belgian government's proposal for these sanctions mechanisms were to be implemented, it would be rejected first and then examined. i hope that sanctions mechanisms will be considered before the biarritz and nice summits.

- mr president, in the enlargement debate there was talk of a very fast timetable. on the other hand, it is required that the new member states adopt and implement the acquis communautaire as a whole before accession and that transitional periods should be few and short. there is an irreconcilable conflict between these positions. some have suggested that this conflict should be resolved by offering the applicant countries eea membership first. this idea is not realistic. the eea is not suitable for the applicant countries, and they will not settle for anything less than membership of the european union. rapid enlargement of the eu is a political necessity. that is why i have asked and i ask again, why not change the terms of membership? we could define a constitution containing the core elements of the eea treaties and the common foreign and security policy. in addition to adopting and implementing this statute, new members would of course have to meet the copenhagen conditions, accept the adoption of the whole acquis as a longer-term objective and commit themselves to the political objectives of the union. in this way we could create the conditions for the accession of most or even all the candidate countries in the coming years. a split of the eu acquis would, on the one hand, safeguard the level of integration achieved by the current member states and, on the other, help to strengthen their cooperation. the rapid enlargement of the union would require a strong decentralisation, as the eldr group's position on enlargement suggests. the union's tasks and competences should therefore be narrowed down and focused on core issues that can only be dealt with at eu level. the union would then also differ in that some of the present member states would go further in integration than others. an enlarged union would be both fragmented and disintegrated. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the information provided by the member states.
- mr president, enlargement is not taking place in a controlled and organised manner according to the timetable proposed by the commission. the negotiations need not be concluded by the end of 2002 for the sake of political ambition if the problems are not resolved by then. we must avoid political haste. the main problem is the different levels of development of the countries, which best illustrates the unification of germany. in germany, the economic, social and cultural differences were not as great as those between many of the current member states and most of the candidate countries, but the process has already cost €600 billion. where are the payers to be found for even greater sums, to bridge even greater development gaps? the idea of a referendum on enlargement, put forward by commissioner verheugen, was rejected for a simple reason: the decision cannot be left to the payers. i would suggest that the model of enlargement is the same as that which was followed in the previous enlargement. the candidate countries will first join the european economic area, where they can integrate their economies into the eu economy without political commitments and constraints.
- mr president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, enlargement across the old iron curtain is a work of peace. peace creates hope, and war destroys it. as we demand legality and the eradication of corruption in the candidate countries, we must also implement the principles of equality and integrity ourselves. the gap between our income and our knowledge has only grown. but enlargement must not be done by first selecting new nato members. that would be following the logic of a war machine. nor can we assume that some applicant is obviously the first and that without it the enlargement could not begin at all. the candidate countries must be equal and their accession must depend solely on their own development. some well-known shortcut providers are like dishonest judges in a sports competition. the union is not prepared to accept new members if our unemployment rate continues to be so high. this inevitably leads to conflict and further imbalances caused by extremism. that's our work station. we must draw the iron curtain between us, because the elimination of social and educational inequalities is a much more important condition than the weighting of votes in the commission or the council. the ignorant and marginalized cannot constitute an information society, but are a sign of its failure. i warn those who are trying to tear the union apart into two: the insiders and the outsiders. we are in the process of ending the division of europe, not creating a new one. the best way to simplify bureaucracy is to focus on the issues that need to be solved together. it is essential that decisions on these common issues be taken in the member states and in the municipalities, otherwise we will be entangled in a fearful control society dominated by an ever darker core. mr president, i propose that the commission should provide parliament with an assessment of the development of the internal conditions for enlargement in the light of unemployment, exclusion and digital divide.
- mr president, we must have a positive movement for enlargement. the environment, among other things, is one of the major benefits of enlargement, along with many other areas. but this requires that we have clear rules on how the expansion is to take place. it is essential for the environment that we have limited options for transitional provisions and that we have a clear indication of how we will proceed during the transitional period. we have a lot of talk about how to finance enlargement. we have the resources allocated in the budget under agenda 2000, but we must ensure that the necessary resources, which are needed particularly in the environmental field, are available precisely before accession. more resources than are currently proposed in agenda 2000 must be directed specifically to pre-enlargement activities. only in this way can we ensure that the legislation is implemented. it is true that the administrative capacity must also be provided for. overall, the costs of enlargement have been very much in the public debate. it was therefore very pleasing to hear that mr klaus hänsch, speaking for the pse group, wished the commission to make a real assessment of the effects of enlargement and also of the economic effects of non-enlargement. let us consider the costs to us if the two-tier system continues and what this means in the social and environmental spheres.
- mr president, the idea put forward today by mr elmar brok of an eea-type arrangement as an alternative for candidate countries which do not meet the copenhagen criteria was relatively little commented on. i think the proposal is worth considering. the eea agreement should not in any case replace full membership and leave the countries in a perpetual waiting room from which there is no way forward, but it could serve as a kind of tool that would reward candidate countries with a rewarding incentive to implement the acquis communautaire. the eea could act as a sort of graveyard where eu babies mature towards full membership. if full membership is granted within a short timeframe, the long transition periods are a major concern for member states' businesses, as they distort competition. on the other hand, the slow pace is also an obvious problem in the face of frustration in the candidate countries. this tension is what makes the eea model so meaningful, because it rewards the applicant at the aspirational stage. this would imply a stronger commitment to the implementation of eu law than is required by the current convergence agreements. the eea, which includes the first pillar, gives access to the internal market to the business community in the candidate countries and to youth and science exchange programmes, but does not oblige the eu to extend its agricultural aid system to these countries. in this way, the eea agreement would provide a positive boost to the economies of the candidate countries - which need growth to get their societies in line - and to the eu economy, with economic growth estimated to cover up to two thirds of the costs of enlargement, and perhaps even more in the eea model. the market in central and eastern europe is said to be so open, thanks to the convergence agreements, that western businessmen can selfishly ask why enlargement is needed. the answer is the implementation of the acquis communautaire. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the market is not opened up to competition. ultimately, we need protection against it ourselves. the eea model is useful because it is based on the idea that the efforts made in implementation are immediately rewarded. the benefits and the liability issues go hand in hand. the eea model, which is a model of membership and a model of reward, would also be fruitful because of the public opinion. the fact that the economic benefits of accession are already apparent before accession itself also provides the basis for the greatest possible justification of enlargement. this applies to citizens of both the candidate and the current member states.
- mr president, i agree that legislation in this area must be comprehensive and inclusive, but as a farmer i cannot agree with everything in that report, such as the fact that feed produced on a farm should also be covered by the provisions. i think it's something that cannot be applied or controlled in practice. we should therefore be working on legislation which is sensible in the sense that it can be both monitored and implemented. the type of legislation which cannot be monitored or implemented in practice naturally erodes the credibility of parliament, as does the fact that we are preparing such legislation. i'll take the 18th amendment as an example. how can you effectively control what and where a pet in the wild drinks? the basic idea is right. legislation must be comprehensive and inclusive, but it must also be practicable and controllable. the application of legislation to feed produced on the farm is not feasible or controllable in practice. otherwise, i am very much in agreement with this overall line and basic idea.
- mr president, the directives in question are part of the union's effort to restore consumer confidence in european food and, at the same time, citizens' confidence in the union's ability to act on this matter, which affects the lives of citizens. marit paulsen has been very much involved in this work, with the report on harmful substances in feedingstuffs and the report on the control of feedingstuffs. it is practically impossible to require that the feed be free from any harmful substances. the maximum levels above which the batch concerned is not suitable for use as feed are now being set. the commission also proposes to eliminate the possibility of diluting a batch of feed exceeding such maximum quantities with a batch not exceeding the limit value. fortunately, this practice has been unknown in the feed industry in many member states until now. the commission's proposal is welcome in this respect and clearly better than the amendment tabled by the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, which would still allow for dilution. the dilution would only lead to unhealthy phenomena in the use and handling of raw materials. the committee also recommends that the commission's proposal be followed in this respect. the only thing i really missed from the committee was a hand reaching out to the producer. the farmer must of course be compensated for the destruction of the contaminated fodder stock which has become unfit for use, as is the case, for example, with the destruction of animals suffering from tse. the compensation is necessary, even though it is clear that farmers themselves are very concerned about the welfare of their animals. the committee also accepted our amendment, which is similar to the split between farm-produced and commercial feed standards. the first is governed by hygiene legislation and the directive on harmful substances in animal feedingstuffs is the most important for the latter. this division should be maintained in the future. if the hygiene legislation is deficient in this respect, it must be revised.
in writing. - (fi) the opening up of the european union to new members will increase stability and prosperity on our continent. it is therefore very positive that the accession negotiations with all the applicant countries have made progress. the candidate countries must be able to join the european union according to their level of development. the principle of differentiation must therefore be applied. the geographical grouping of the applicant countries and the territorial staggering of the accession dates cannot be approved. enlargement will also benefit the current members of the european union. the political benefits and macroeconomic gains of enlargement will outweigh the additional costs to member states. the european union is already benefiting from the growing trade surplus in its trade relations with the candidate countries. it would be desirable for the governments of the candidate countries to continue to strengthen their administrative capacity, so that the community acquis can be applied in these countries before accession. the great progress made by estonia in preparing for accession is a very positive thing for finland in particular. our neighbour has made a determined effort over the past decade to meet the criteria for membership. the law on languages, which is important for the minority issue in estonia, came into force in june 2000 and complies with all international standards. the process of economic transition has also progressed positively. it is desirable for all parties to find rapid solutions to the remaining issues. the estonian government should therefore give greater weight to the fight against corruption and drug trafficking.

- mr president, i think that the report by mr robert j. evans is very important and i voted in favour of it. the right of free movement of european citizens continues to be violated in many ways. students in particular often face obstacles and difficulties in the member states that should no longer exist under eu law. the european commission's investigations show that students from other eu countries are made to live and study in an unnecessarily burdensome way. residence permits are issued for very short periods and are difficult and expensive to renew. unfortunately, there are some unfortunate cases. in spain, students are required to deposit - specifically in a spanish bank - special security deposits in order to obtain a rental apartment. the transfer of students' family members has also been made more difficult. it has also recently become clear that some london rental housing agencies do not accept a foreign guarantor, for example a finnish guarantor, to guarantee the rent payment of a finnish student. the european citizens' rights do not seem to matter much in this situation.
in writing. - (fi) as regards amendment no 18, the directive in question concerns equal treatment in employment and in the general framework of the profession, and the wording, which is too general, does not fit in all its details within the framework of this directive. it is clear from the grounds for the amendment that the directive is intended to intervene in the field of the age regulations applied in the member states, regulations which are nevertheless quite justified on grounds of genetic psychology and have no impact on working life. for these reasons i voted against the amendment, although we must oppose all forms of discrimination in employment and professional life. as regards amendment no 37, the wording of article 4.2 of the directive proposed by the committee is good and may make it possible to ensure in a significant way the continuity of the extensive social work carried out by religious associations. this work has formed the basis of all sociopolitical thought in europe and still represents an important part of practical action. in addition, the wording proposed by the committee reinforces the vitality of the culture and customs of small communities, especially those of immigrants. for these reasons i voted in favour of the amendment.
the rapporteur. - mr president, the european investment bank provides loans to non-eu countries which are guaranteed by the european union. the investment bank is free to grant individual loans according to its own criteria without specific eu guidelines. however, the union has put a ceiling on the loans it has guaranteed. the loans are not guaranteed at 100%, but the council decided in december 1999 to guarantee only 65% of the total amount of loans for the period 2000-2007. the remainder must be secured by other collateral or risked by the investment bank itself. in practice, there is no risk and the european investment bank would even be willing to reduce the percentage of the eu guarantee. the union budget contains a special guarantee fund for loans from the investment bank. it shall be financed by a specific reserve, the maximum amount of which shall be decided in the financial envelope contained in the interinstitutional agreement. in 2000, the reserve may be transferred to the guarantee fund up to a maximum of eur 204 million. technically, for loans from the investment bank, 9% of the amount, which is 65% of the nominal value of the loans, is transferred from the reserve to the guarantee fund. the budget guarantee fund is also used for so-called macro-financial assistance to non-eu countries. it's a cheap way for member countries to give development aid. these loans are granted on political grounds to humble eu partners and are guaranteed by the guarantee fund, unlike loans from investment banks, which are guaranteed at 100%. macroeconomic assistance is often a question of the eu's contribution to an overall package in which the world bank and the international monetary fund are the other donors. in general, financial assistance to third countries is granted according to the economic and political behavioural criteria set by the international monetary fund - the more guarantees are given to investment bank loans, the less macro-financial assistance can be given and vice versa. the guarantee fund also guarantees so-called euratom loans to support the development of the nuclear industry in third countries, but in recent years no transfers from the reserves to the guarantee fund have been necessary for these purposes. in december 1999, the council fixed the ceiling for guarantees to be granted to the investment bank for the period 2000-07 and drew up a list of eligible third countries. the european parliament took a position on this in the rühle report in autumn 1999. after consulting parliament, the matter shall no longer be dealt with in parliament as regards the conditions of the guarantees. the decision before parliament today is a very simple one, to include croatia among the countries to which the european investment bank may grant loans with an eu guarantee. there are no more wars in croatia, and there has been a democratic change. the commission notes that the loan was granted to the land of thuringia in the form of a loan for the construction of a new airport in the region of thuringia. if parliament could amend the council decision, it would certainly recall the unemployment in croatia and the social problems caused by the weight loss programme for croatia, but in this context there is no legal basis for such amendments. the same legal jurisdiction problem concerns the decision to be discussed in the near future in parliament on the inclusion of turkey among the beneficiaries of eu loan guarantees to the european investment bank. parliament would probably have many conditions it would like to impose on the turkish loans, but even then it will decide in practice on only one word. today, the beneficiaries of the guarantee are added to croatia, then turkey. in this context, we also inform the commission that next year the guarantee fund will not be able to guarantee the loans that are available.
- mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the report by the committee on budgets which we are now considering, which recommends extending the community guarantee to the european investment bank to loans granted in croatia, points to two important points. firstly, the political situation in croatia has improved considerably. the new government has committed itself to political and economic reforms. it will enable the european union to continue the cooperation that was interrupted in 1995 by the armed actions launched by croatia in krajina. the second message is that croatia is still in a very difficult economic situation. the country has been a predatory economy for years, leaving the new government with a crippled business sector and unemployment of over 20 percent. the rapporteur, esko seppänen, rightly points out in the explanatory statement to his report that the social conditions in croatia are difficult and that the community's support for the reform process should also have a positive impact on the social dimension. a major political change took place in croatia with the parliamentary elections held earlier this year. the people of the country voted to demand democratic change. the new government was formed by a democratic front of six parties with a solid majority in the legislature. the government has been quick to show its recognition of the values of european democracy and the rule of law. in its external relations, it gives priority to integration into euro-atlantic structures. the programme commits the government to respect for human and minority rights, the return of refugees, improved neighbourly relations and substantial economic reforms, and legislation has already been adopted to achieve these objectives. developments in croatia have led to a rapid rapprochement of eu-croatian relations after years of absence. the union and croatia have established, inter alia, an advisory working group to provide croatia with expertise and technical assistance in the negotiation of contractual relations. the commission considers that negotiations on a stabilisation and association agreement with the country should be opened as soon as possible. in august, the council decided to extend the tempus 3 programme for higher education to croatia. in july, the commission released eur 10 million to support the return of refugees. the proposal under consideration includes a target of eur 250 million in eib loans to croatia for the period 2000-2007. the amount is intended to finance basic infrastructure projects and environmental investments, the development of industry and smes, housing reconstruction, urban renewal and tourism. the plans are in line with the projects proposed by the croatian regional finance conference in march. the croatian authorities have committed themselves to a difficult but necessary economic reform. the situation is difficult, but it is hoped that the loans now under consideration will help to alleviate the negative effects of economic reform on the lives of citizens. it should also be noted that croatia has good industrial facilities, a skilled and educated workforce and excellent opportunities for the development of, for example, tourism. the commission considers that a series of coherent and ambitious restructuring measures could significantly strengthen the country's competitiveness on the european market, but at present many croatian companies still have difficulties in competing in an open economy. the events in the balkans and the future of the region are very topical issues. this week we have been watching with bated breath the situation in yugoslavia after the presidential elections. the winds of change are blowing in belgrade too. the international community has already expressed its support for the serbian people and sanctions are being lifted. the european union and the european parliament have every reason to support the positive developments in the balkans. the instability in the region is a threat to all of europe. we know that nationalism and poverty mean war. there is a great deal of material and spiritual reconstruction ahead, and we too must do our part in removing the obstacles to effective relief. unfortunately, the negotiations between the institutions on the next year's union budget - and in particular on the financing of heading iv - are still proving very difficult at this stage. i hope that the signs of hope in croatia and now also in yugoslavia will encourage us to find a solution to the problems of the budget and its financial prospects.
mr president, commissioner, mr evans and mr karas, in this debate, have made a good point about state aid and competition policy. it should be borne in mind that, for example, in the context of internal competition, national subsidies are particularly important and valuable in countries with a small population and long distances. i would like to mention one particular point in this context, namely transport subsidies. take my home country, for example, with its long distances. the price of fuel in northern finland is eur 0.2 higher than in southern finland. in addition, transport of raw materials, transport of feed and the like are particularly important and key issues for internal competition in order to allow state aid to continue in these areas. they are of particular importance for regional economy and employment, and i hope that this will be made clear in the future so that these aids, internal aids to regional development and the regional economy, of which transport aids are a prime example, can continue.
mr president, commissioner monti, ladies and gentlemen, an open, competitive and coordinated european market economy requires an effective competition policy. without a properly managed competition policy, the european union's economy, and its external expression, the euro, will never be able to reach its full potential. only a company that is competitive in the internal market without state aid can be competitive on the world market. however, when designing european competition policy, care must be taken to ensure that competition policy does not restrict the ability of firms to succeed in the wider global market. as mrs riis-jørgensen rightly emphasizes in her report and in her speech, there is a danger, particularly in the case of small member states, that the definition of dominant market position will be based on a too narrow conception of the market, making it more difficult for companies in small member states to become sufficiently large players in the global competitive environment. moreover, a dominant position is not even a problem unless there is evidence that it is being abused. it is only when a dominant position leads to a disadvantage for consumers and competitors that strict measures must be taken, as you have done, mr monti. as pointed out in paragraph 15 of mrs riis-jørgensen's report, the focus must be on the harm caused by restrictive measures rather than on the nature of the economic operators concerned. special attention must be paid to the situation of small and medium-sized enterprises. small state aid may be more harmful to an sme than expected if the market is small. it is therefore good that this point is also emphasized in the riis-jørgensen report. in his report, mr evans thanks you, mr monti, for your firm stance on state aid policy. large state aid remains a problem in many sectors. although state aid may appear to be a good solution on the surface, in the longer term, state aid very rarely has lasting positive effects. i don't want to condemn all state aid. there are also necessary subsidies in line with the treaties. in the long term, however, the level of state aid must be reduced. state aid delays the necessary structural changes, distorts competition and fuels inefficiency. in a way, state subsidies are keeping the potemkin backdrop alive, which will eventually collapse. in such cases, the payer is the private consumer and the taxpayer.
mr president, mr moscovici, mr barnier, although the biarritz summit did not make progress on the key issue of structural reform of the union, i am confident that the presidency of the council, led by the innovative french president chirac, will also make structural decisions in nice that will carry the current of development forward and be acceptable to all. however, there is concern that the voting weightings on some issues were threatening. the media spread the idea of the contradictions between small and large countries to the citizens of europe. the union cannot develop into a community of 27 countries if it is maintained as a principle of intergovernmental cooperation, with the union's bodies merely implementing or exercising control. there are already numerous examples of how the priorities of almost all countries are being held back by one country. the responsibility for such is shared by countries large and small alike. examples include the european company, the pan-european liberalization and privatisation of the postal service, the opening up of competition in the energy sector and the start of tax harmonisation. the warning example of misconduct is also given, surprisingly, by austria, which has just experienced ill-treatment itself when it threatens its neighbour the czech republic with the commissioning of the temelin nuclear power plant. the union now needs not visionaries with a vision for 2030, but decision-makers who can see the problems that enlargement will bring in the next 3 to 8 years and the answers to them. the solutions must be found within the institutions of the union, by finding the flexibility to allow some of the union to cooperate more closely with each other, if necessary, by strengthening the role of the commission and by adopting a principle of qualified majority voting. the charter of fundamental rights is a splendid expression of european values, but it is useless to make a contrived argument about its rapid, binding constitutionalisation. here too, decisions must be made over time, including in the nordic countries.
the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism has drawn up an opinion. mr president, i too would like to thank the rapporteur, mr haug, for the excellent work he has done in preparing the budget. he has visited our committee and has taken the proposals of our committee very much into account. the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism aims to improve employment by supporting smes in particular and by exploiting the potential of the information society. the committee stresses discipline and sound financial management, simplification of administrative procedures and the effectiveness of projects. the committee supports the commission's proposals and rejected the council's cuts in payment appropriations as unjustified. our aim is to deal quickly with old problems and to launch new programmes quickly and efficiently. the transport sector will focus on the development of transeuropean transport networks. the appropriations for road safety, sustainable transport and combined transport are limited to symbolic appropriations. i would hope that this will improve in the coming years, as the accident rate varies surprisingly much between member states, and for example the exchange of good transport practices should be increased. the community should also contribute to the development of the tourism sector by supporting smes in this sector in particular. our committee believes that the commission should present a new programme to support tourism. the ispa support for preparing for enlargement should co-finance the launching of projects in order to make efficient use of limited resources.

behalf of my group, the position of the committee on budgets on next year's budget.

mr president, within the established power structures, nothing new is ever born. the banning of antipersonnel mines has led to a process which is an excellent example of how non-governmental movements, ngos and the european parliament have been able to define and pursue with determination objectives which state actors have set aside or deemed unrealistic. after the immediate breakthrough, it is now necessary to ensure that the union and its member states take the necessary follow-up both in their bilateral relations and in their internal legislation and maintain a leading role in strengthening and maintaining the political will to ensure that the agreement is signed and ratified on a comprehensive basis, including in my home country, finland. it must also be made a precondition for eu membership for the applicant countries, i.e. incorporated into the acquis communautaire. the report we have before us, and in particular the excellent opinion of the committee on development, provide a good basis for further action, which must focus on actual de-mining and its operational implementation rather than on the search for exotic and costly technological solutions. biosensors are proving to be very promising in this respect. the brok resolution, in turn, provides the political dimension for this work and the oral questions to the commission by mr brok and mrs miranda give the commission a guideline which is essential. all eu institutions must take the initiative and ensure that the poorest countries most affected by the mine problem are effectively included in the programme. the commission has the primary responsibility for monitoring, of course, but we all have a part to play in ensuring that the joint efforts to eradicate this scourge worldwide are completed as quickly as possible. we can leave a world without mines behind.
mr president, the last time the finnish government dealt with the issue of landmines was on 14 june 1999. it was decided to initiate a study aimed at enabling finland to accede to the ottawa treaty in 2006 and to replace the landmines by other methods by the end of 2010. finns participate in international cooperation on mine clearance. finnish experts and finnish demolition vehicles have been involved in the demolition of cambodia for two and a half years, mozambique for one year and kosovo for one year. as a contracting party, finland shall comply with the provisions of the 1996 revised mine ban protocol to the convention on conventional weapons, which entered into force in december 1998. finland does not feel itself threatened militarily. but we are the only eu country to have more than a thousand kilometres of land border with a foreign power. finland shall take care of its own credible defence. until a replacement weapon system is procured, which costs about half of finland's one-year defense budget, infantry mines are part of the defense system. there are no landmines in finland. finland has never exported anti-personnel mines and none have been produced in finland since 1981. all the mines are in storage. in finland, since the post-world war ii rage period that ended in the 1940s, finnish infantry mines have not caused any harm to civilians or finnish soldiers. unfortunately, finland is one of the few countries in the world that signs and ratifies international agreements only when it is able to fulfil their terms to the last detail. this will also be the case with landmines.
in writing. - (fi) finland intends to sign the ottawa convention in 2006 and replace antipersonnel mines with other weapons systems by 2010. finland has never, on any occasion, exported antipersonnel mines. finland has produced and stored antipersonnel mines solely for its own defence. finland's defence expenditure is traditionally among the lowest in europe. the decision to abandon the use of antipersonnel mines poses considerable challenges to finnish national defence. landmines are weapons of self-defence. or can someone tell me how to attack with anti-personnel mines? mines are central to finland's defence strategy. alternative programme solutions are planned to be considered in the new defence debate by 2005. further work will focus on issues of funding, alternative systems and processes and the impacts of the solutions chosen on the evolution of defence doctrine. finland continues its participation in the goal of total prohibition and in international cooperation on mine clearance.

draftsman of the opinion of the committee on legal affairs and the internal market. mr president, mr commissioner, i would first like to thank the rapporteur for really bringing together all our views and doing a great job. emissions trading came into the climate debate as part of so-called flexible mechanisms. the european union did not want to include them in the alternative package, but they are now in an international agreement and the rules for these so-called flexible mechanisms, of which emissions trading is one of the key ones, are being drawn up in the hague. the eu has been the leading force in these international negotiations up to now, and we all certainly hope that this leadership will continue and we have confidence in this commission. the european union has therefore decided to propose that the transition to greenhouse gas emissions trading within the union should begin as early as 2005, which is different from the fact that the kyoto treaty itself allows for such international emissions trading as of 2008. but within the european union, this system can be tested and developed. the committee on legal affairs therefore welcomes the commission's green paper and the fact that it will be the basis for a framework directive. action at european union level on emissions trading is absolutely justified, otherwise competition will be distorted. i would like to draw the attention of the committee to the fact that emissions trading can do a lot, but not everything. it is very important that emissions trading should complement other means, of which economic controls are the key. it is also very important that the emissions trading is monitored effectively and i believe that we also need environmental inspectors, which we are currently negotiating with the council.
mr president, the two reports by mr moreira da silva which are before us are important in the preparation of the community for the meeting of the parties to the kyoto agreement next month. they are seeking a leading role for the eu in the international fight to slow down climate change. i believe that the work of moreira da silva has contributed to this objective. according to the european environment agency, eu greenhouse gas emissions are expected to increase by 6% between 1990 and 2010. it also estimates that the transport sector will be the one with the highest growth in emissions. urban transport in particular plays a key role in reducing co2 emissions from transport. the committee therefore encourages the commission to make proposals in its new green paper to change the transport culture in developing countries in order to achieve a lasting reduction in greenhouse gas emissions, and considers it necessary to increase support for public transport. the committee criticised the commission for the fact that policy and measures are secondary to the eu's strategy to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. this cannot be accepted if the eu is to play a leading role in slowing down climate change. the eu's own contribution is necessary, in particular, because the outcome of the sixth conference of the parties cannot yet be guaranteed. mr president, i want to make this very simple. i feel that we politicians have not yet realised the seriousness of our situation with regard to climate change. as politicians, we've been accustomed to making compromises about almost everything, and we've learned that the world is shaped like that, the shape of compromise. but now we have a party with whom we cannot negotiate, nature is a hard fact, it does not submit to a contract, it just is. this is so simple that it's almost embarrassing to say it, but this is, i believe, the worst confusion point in our thinking.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, although the priority of the union's climate policy must be sustainable environmental policy, we must also take into account the international competitiveness of industry and the smooth functioning of the internal market in our decisions. the responsibility for implementing the kyoto protocol and the union's burden-sharing obligations lies with the states. the ability of countries to take action to reduce emissions varies widely. if country quotas were allocated across the eu to different sectors, as mr blokland said, for example, when he presented the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, companies with similar activities would be placed in a completely unequal position in different member states. even if a particular sector in one member state is among the best in the world in terms of environmental performance - and this is true of many industries in finland, for example - it may be subject to a stricter emission quota than its less efficient competitors in another country. this would inevitably lead to distortions of competition and therefore no quantitative targets should be set at community level for the various sectors of the economy. it is essential to promote measures which are practicable and do not jeopardise the competitiveness of enterprises. my country, finland, is the only eu country, besides france, which is currently considering further development of nuclear power. i warmly support the further development of nuclear power, as this would give our energy-intensive industry a competitive edge in terms of both economy and climate emissions. the report also does not therefore advocate or specifically oppose the use of individual forms of electricity generation, but rather calls for the use of all forms of electricity generation in the formulation of national climate strategies. i would also like to stress the importance of voluntary, negotiated agreements on objectives. they have been considered in several member states as a very effective means of contributing to the achievement of the climate objective. voluntary agreements could be a good mechanism, especially in the enlargement process.
