madam president, i would like to thank you. the charter contains several welcome points. the right to good administration, which malmström also mentioned, is one of the most important advances. that right is not contained in other international instruments, and it is absent from many constitutions of the member states. access to a more open administration is one of the most tangible improvements that the charter can offer our eu citizens. the charter also opens up the way for minority rights, since article 22 states that the union shall respect cultural, religious and linguistic diversity. this respect, combined with the prohibition of discrimination, creates a basis for safeguarding the position of minorities in the future. the working method has been successful. it has been so successful that the finnish prime minister, in his speech last friday, identified the method as a way of coming up with ideas for a future constitution for the eu. i think this is important. however, it is also important that the eu adheres to the human rights conventions - the two are interrelated. finally, i am pleased to note that the children are also mentioned. i hope that this will provide a basis for commissioner vitorino to take action so that the eu can make a contribution to the forthcoming summit on the situation of children in the autumn. i am also very proud to note that a representative of my parliamentary group in my home country, gunnar jansson, played a very important role in the drafting of this charter. i think he has contributed to the clarity that berès mentioned here. the charter is clear and concise, and an example for many constitutions.
the rapporteur. - mr president, mr minister, mr commissioner, we have two reports in the debate today, of which the morillon report is more political, whereas my report is more technical. the council proposes that turkey be included among the countries eligible for loans from the european investment bank, with the european union guaranteeing these loans. the council is proposing unanimously to include turkey among the beneficiaries of the guarantees, and the parliament's committee on budgets has also been unanimous on this point. the consensus does not mean that we are all happy with democracy in turkey and with respect for human rights in turkey; we are not. the european union is not a party to the treaty of rome, but it is a party to the treaty of rome. the european union has accepted turkey as one of the countries with which membership negotiations are taking place in helsinki, and the human rights situation in turkey is not just an internal matter. turkey's application for membership has given the union the right to take a position on its own internal affairs. the temptation to impose conditions resembling those of democracy on turkish loans has been strong, but in the case of this report there has been no technical possibility of doing so. it is simply a supplement to the previous council decision 2000/2024/ec. if conditions were to be imposed, they should have been imposed in a decision taken earlier last year. at that time, it was not known that turkey would be named as a beneficiary of the guarantees following the positive development of the accession negotiations. moreover, the conditions could not have been imposed by a decision of the european parliament alone. i would point out how little parliament can influence the activities of the european investment bank and how poorly the european union generally supervises the activities of the bank. the eu budget guarantee fund, which guarantees loans to turkey, is compulsory expenditure and the council has therefore not had to take parliament's positions into account. in the european parliament we would like to impose conditions on loans granted by the european investment bank in general, conditions relating to the environment and the social protection of third country nationals, and in the case of turkey, conditions relating to respect for human rights. but the current legal basis of the guarantee fund does not make that possible. better cooperation between the eu institutions is needed. the problems of supervision and audit of the management of the eib are also due to the fact that its governing bodies are composed exclusively of representatives of the member states, some of whom wish to prevent the commission and the european parliament from taking a position on the bank's affairs. the european investment bank is thus one of the last remaining safe havens for banking in the world. there, the waves of social discourse do not slip. finally, i would like to draw the attention of the commission to something that it knows - and i would therefore like to draw the attention of the council to this in particular - that in 2001 the guarantee fund will not be able to cover all the commitments made. this will be the case in 2002. if the commitments are respected, the ceilings for guarantees will have to be revised. it is the only subject on which parliament has a legitimate say. the council is extending loans and guarantees beyond the legal framework.
- mr president, commissioner, the rapporteur, mr garot, has dealt with this commission proposal for a community fund in a very substantial and detailed manner. the aim of balancing production and marketing in advance is in the common interest of the production chain and of all concerned. i would stress, however, that we should not go into a regulatory economy in the pig economy at this stage. i very much agree with mr buski when he said that we are facing enlargement to the east, and in a situation like this, where we will have to intervene very seriously in the whole of agricultural policy in a few years' time, i do not think it is worth going to the regulatory economy for one sector, it would not be wise now. the south and the north are clearly opposed here: the south is more in favour of cofinancing, while the northern small-scale economy is based on the idea that it should be very strongly based on voluntary work, and on the basis of voluntary work it is possible to set up schemes in which - as schierhuber said here a moment ago - small-scale farmers from all over europe could participate. the community could be financing mainly technically, technical matters at the time of the start-up, and thereby supporting the start-up of the system. i understand that in the future voluntary funds will also help to balance markets in the pig sector.
- mr president, i too would like to thank mr blokland for the preparation of this important matter. the drafting of the directive has been a difficult process because of the different circumstances and practices in the member states and the many different interest groups involved. the result is a fairly balanced proposal which sets strict limits on the incineration of both hazardous and non-hazardous waste, improves the state of the environment and makes it possible to use waste also for the production of electricity and heat. one of the most difficult things was to define what was called co-incineration. in some countries, the best environmental alternative is to burn carefully sorted combustible waste in small municipal plants that generate electricity and heat. the first drafts required these institutions to carry out unnecessary, technically expensive and sometimes impossible measurements. during the process, the performance has improved. it is also important that industry can continue to use non-hazardous waste for its own energy production. i also think that the problem was that the directive went into such technical detail that it was difficult to get an unambiguous opinion even from the experts. i think that parliament should stick to the definition of broad guidelines, which would allow member states to take account of different circumstances and to continue to apply best environmental practices. the waste incineration directive increases the cost of waste incineration, but reduces harmful emissions and improves the state of the environment. the debate in parliament has produced a reasonably balanced proposal which can serve as a basis for action throughout the union.
- mr president, i would like to thank mr blokland in particular for his cooperation during this process. the negotiation of the exceptions to the scope of the directive was one of the most difficult issues in the deliberations on the directive and also in the conciliation. the aim was to limit the exceptions strictly and to define them as clearly as possible. i think that we have succeeded in this, in that the ecological objectives and the compatibility with the other community policies are being achieved in a way that is even more easily achievable. parliament also pushed for greater transparency and improved access to information for the public. the conciliation thus allowed for the publication of an annual report on the emissions of installations burning more than 2 tonnes per hour and for the inclusion of smaller combustion plants in lists open to the public. the emission limit values in the annex to the directive were certainly the most difficult part of the directive. the compromise reached in this respect was also a success, with the adoption of parliament's proposal for stricter nitrogen oxide emission limits for all new cement kilns for co-incineration. i myself managed to get some amendments in the report which were vital for the continuation of the co-incineration practices which were adopted in finland years ago and which are even more ecological than the directive which is now being adopted. i am very pleased about that because as a new member, directly elected to the second reading, the task was not easy. the report by mr blokland, now in its third reading, was also the first real experience for many of the members of the committee on the environment who were elected to parliament for the first time, and the first experience in which they learned about parliament's practices. but the procedures adopted in the institutions work surprisingly well. the outcome can reasonably be considered good. however, the genuine willingness of all three institutions to cooperate was a major factor. the portuguese and french presidencies' activities were constructive and above all the commission's role, which was crucial in bringing about a good outcome, should be mentioned. i think we must learn from this for the future and ensure that cooperation works for a greener european union.
- mr president, the dependence of the member states of the union on imported energy is high and is increasing all the time. dependence on fossil fuels is dependence on imported energy and on foreign policy decisions. the use of renewable energy sources increases the freedom and independence of peoples and is compatible with the principles of ecologically and socially sustainable development. the report draws a line in the sand as to what constitutes renewable energy sources. without prejudice to the question whether peat is a renewable energy resource, peat must be capable of being used as a fuel for energy production, preferably in combined heat and power (chp). turf is primarily in the nordic countries, and we must have the possibility of burning it in our countries, because it is an energy resource of its own and reduces the eu's dependence on imports. it produces carbon dioxide when it burns, but it produces it when we burn fossil fuels. the countries that burn oil or gas themselves should not forbid us to burn peat. if we don't burn peat, we burn oil or gas. in our country, we are not told that burning peat reduces carbon dioxide, but peat is a fuel of its own, whereas oil and gas are imported. waste is not a renewable resource, although more and more is being generated every day. gas from landfills may be burned, but the amount of waste should not be increased to allow for further burning of gas.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i thank mr rothe for his excellent report and for his understanding. of course, it is desirable to increase the use of electricity from renewable sources. the aim is therefore to double that share in ten years; it could be more. however, the union's dependence on imported energy is now becoming too great. in a couple of decades, it's up to 70 percent. it creates uncertainty in our lives, both economically and otherwise. i think that we need a modern energy conservation programme alongside the development of renewable energy sources, because this dependence is also becoming a security issue for us. the peat keeps piling up. the formation of peat mass during contact and shrimp production is similar to that of other plant biomass. instead of taking 100 years, like a tree, peat takes thousands of years to regenerate. we need peat, especially in the east and north of my country, where all fuel except wood is imported from far away. we don't want to burn and be dependent on coal and oil alone. we've got a very good annual growth rate in peat. that's a lot, too. we finns consider both forests and wetlands to be almost sacred and we know how to use them in accordance with the principle of sustainable development.
- mr president, i would like to thank my colleague mr rothe for the valuable work he has done in drawing up this report. the promotion of the use of electricity from renewable energy sources is highly desirable. in any case, the eu's dependence on imported energy will increase significantly over the next 20 years. the eu must also stick to the kyoto climate targets, so it is particularly important to invest more in research into renewable energy sources. i am pleased that the new directive takes account of the importance of biomass, peat and other wood-based raw materials, among other things. the debate on renewable energy sources must not turn into populism against nuclear power. it should be remembered that only the use of nuclear power in conjunction with renewable energy sources will enable the kyoto climate agreement to be fulfilled. the debate on nuclear energy in sweden and germany, among others, has shown that without environmentally friendly and competitively priced energy alternatives in sight, the debate will inevitably remain deadlocked. full liberalisation of the electricity market is also a precondition for increasing the use of renewable energy sources. the artificial barriers to trade and restrictions on trade maintained by monopoly companies must be removed, for example, cheap electricity produced by nordic hydropower is not yet able to enter the central european market without obstacles.
- mr president, i too would like to thank the rapporteur for the excellent work he has done on this report. the collaboration has produced a very good performance. the definition of renewable energy sources adopted by the committee is a fairly successful and comprehensive one, and it must also include turf, as many colleagues have convincingly argued here. increasing the use of renewable energy can reduce dependence on oil and create jobs, including in under-resourced areas. it is also an important means of reducing the community's greenhouse gas emissions. i think it is good that the targets are set high, but the most important thing is that common objectives can be agreed in a credible and also binding way, as the rapporteur here earlier referred to. i think it is a good principle that the directive creates a framework for increasing the use of renewable energy sources in electricity production. while the decision and implementation of promotion measures is left to the member states, an increased contribution from the union is also needed.

draftsman of the opinion of the committee on legal affairs and the internal market. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the right of access to documents of the institutions of the european union is a prerequisite for citizens to be able to follow and influence the decisionmaking process. we are now creating an open and modern administration, the natural part of which is that citizens have this direct right. of course, this right must be limited, and that is what we are discussing today. the european union already has a policy of public access to documents, and it is worth remembering that courageous citizens and many progressive member states have been fighting for years for greater openness. we are not starting this debate from scratch, but we can consider it our duty to increase transparency and to meet the criteria required by the amsterdam treaty. the report we are now considering is a step in the right direction in many respects. it is very good that the report states that the public access to documents and the obligation to disclose documents applies to all the institutions, not just the three main ones. it is also very good that the institutions are obliged, or rather given the possibility, to disclose documents also to persons who do not reside in the territory of the european union or are not citizens of the european union. this principle should be further strengthened: ladies and gentlemen, you can well imagine that some day a citizen of the european union will need to know, for example, what the us administration is doing. in fact, there is no reason to restrict the geographical distribution of documents. however, the report does not sufficiently clarify that any separate rules which remain in force on the entry into force of this regulation meet the criteria for greater transparency. this is not clear, and it is possible that more restrictive rules may also remain in force, and we have tabled amendments on this point, among other things. as for the definition of a document, it is a very important question and one that could certainly be debated for a whole year, and certainly has been. it is essential that the institutions be given a certain amount of freedom to exercise what is known as their internal thinking, but we must be very careful because we may create a situation in which the preparatory documents leading to decisions are closed to the public and, of course, there is no possibility of influencing the decision-making process. i think the report also goes too far in recognising the right of so-called third parties to restrict access to documents. it may be the duty of the council and the commission to defend such restrictions, but i do not think that is in the interest of parliament or the citizens. the list of exceptions, as maij-weggen said, is a very important, perhaps the most important part of this work. it has gone much better, but i would like to draw particular attention to the fact that if we want the public to be able to follow the general development of security policy in the future, the exception relating to defence, security and military matters is far too broad. it should be defined more precisely or it should be accompanied by a harm test. mr president, the right of members of parliament to have access to classified documents is a very important matter, but it cannot replace the direct right of citizens to information. this report also requires further work in parliament, and i therefore believe that it must be improved here as well.
the committee on petitions has been asked to submit an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the background to this report has been very wisely stated. i think we should emphasize that this is a matter of turning things upside down. it is now a question of giving the citizens the power to control the institutions. the opinion of the committee on petitions mentions, among other things, the importance of registers. registers are essential if we are not to have a publicity that is an insider publicity. the committee would like to see all documents covered, whether they are drawn up by the institutions or come into the possession of the institutions. it is true that the institutions can be given time to reflect, but that does not mean that the document will never be published. after that period, those documents must also be capable of being assessed. we must also make the balancing act mentioned by the rapporteur, mr cashman. the interests of public debate and the interests of protecting individuals, for example, must always be weighed against each other. it is also important that stakeholders have rights. we stress that they must have greater rights than the general public. however, the issue now is not parliament's access to documents but the public's. the committee on petitions calls for shorter waiting times before documents are released. time is power in our society. we also stress the important role played by the ombudsman in the progress that has been made. i think he will play an important role in the future. we also want to emphasize that this is not a question of harmonizing national rules. national cultures must be allowed to live on, for example in the country from which i come, where the whole principle of public access to documents was once invented.
- mr. speaker, i want to thank cashman for a job well done. however, i am not the only one to thank him, but i also have a special thanks to mrs hanja maij-weggen of the ppe-de group, who, through the strengthened hughes procedure, is the co-author of the report. i am glad that they have been able to work together in a good and productive way. transparency and democracy are important values in our work and they are realized if registers of existing information, including those declared secret, are made compulsory. this is the principle which i have taken as a starting point in my own report on access to environmental information. it is also important that citizens are informed when a document which is not publicly available at the time of the request due to incompleteness or confidentiality is available. i would like to draw attention to the issue of the public disclosure of information held by bodies under the commission or set up by the eu institutions. the european environment agency in copenhagen is one such example, as is the soon to be established food safety authority. naturally, the documents of these services must be included in the publicity rules in an unambiguous way. but i don't think the technique cashman's chosen is a good one. the listing of these units in a separate annex is technically cumbersome when new units are created or changed. it would be better to create the rules themselves, which would oblige all departments to publish their documents and to create registers for them. secondly, i would remind you of the work on exceptions. the introduction of exceptions is a very sensitive matter, because even a good report and a great deal of transparency is easily overshadowed in the minds of the citizens by a clumsy exception clause. naturally, a decision must finally be taken on the practices that will bring the council's work to the attention of the public, and the exception clause must not give the council the opportunity to retreat behind it as it has done in the past. but it is equally important to reduce the public attitudes that are directly akin to paranoia, which are fuelled, for example, by the excessive secrecy of documents relating to security and defence. in fact, it is also important for security that citizens have the opportunity to assess their confidence in european defence through facts and documents.
- mr president, we are now in a position where we can truly say that steps are being taken in the direction that has been demanded for years and whose principles were accepted in the amsterdam treaty. transparency must become the cornerstone of all european union action, but the right of access to documents is not in itself sufficient to achieve transparency. we must ensure that the functioning of all the institutions is based on transparency, clarity and comprehensibility, and that means that the decisionmaking structures are clearly visible. the commission has also been asked to provide information on the implementation of the directive. the time limits proposed for the submission of documents must be maximum periods, since in a normal situation documents are naturally accessible to citizens. i also think it is very important that this report should include an annual review, because the annual review will then justifiably include lists of documents which could not be made public. at this stage it is possible to examine, also on political grounds, whether these assessments of the classification of documents have been appropriate. i hope that this report will be well received here in parliament.
in writing. - (fi) the european union's objective is to double the share of renewable energy sources in energy consumption by 2010. renewable energy is an essential element of an effective environmental protection strategy. peat is an important source of renewable energy in finland, accounting for 6% of the fuels used. unlike the countries further south in europe, in a country like finland there is a lot of peat. according to the international experts themselves, peat is an important biomass fuel, with annual growth well above consumption in the case of finland. the northernmost regions of finland are part of the tundra zone, where wood is not sufficient to be used as an energy source. in these areas in particular, peat is a fuel of great importance which also plays a significant role as a provider of employment. i welcome the fact that the european parliament has recognised these realities by including peat among the renewable energy sources in the report. the eu member states, including finland, have committed themselves to the kyoto targets, to the preservation of natural resources and to the reduction of emissions of harmful substances. for these reasons, nuclear energy is becoming increasingly important. it goes without saying that the exploitation of nuclear energy and the storage of radioactive waste must comply with more modern and more efficient safety standards. the finnish energy company teollisuuden voima submitted to the government yesterday, 15 november, an application for a decision in principle on the construction of a fifth nuclear power station. i hope that our government and our parliament will respond positively to this request. responsible nuclear energy production provides an environmentally friendly and economically viable source of energy, the use of which should not be restricted.

(applause) i would like to thank the we are certainly in agreement, commissioner, that europe will never face a banking crisis as deep as the one we experienced in the early 1990s. however, the banking sector will continue to suffer losses. when a bank is faced with losses, its own funds act as a buffer against losses that cannot be covered by normal operating profits. the proposal now before us is therefore a key instrument in ensuring the solvency of european banks and, therefore, the protection of customers' savings. it is essential that own funds requirements take account as far as possible of the nature and extent of the risks of the bank. modern risk management techniques can provide more accurate information on risks. it is therefore natural that banks should also be given access to these resources and that a more risk-sensitive environment should be created. in order to increase risk sensitivity, both internal and external assessment methods of banks shall be available. in fact, i believe that internal ratings will play an increasingly important role in the future, because often it is within banks that the key people in trustworthy companies and the company's operations are known. there is a great deal of agreement in parliament on the substance of this proposal. in fact, the only significant disagreement between the political groups is about the approach that should be taken to this proposal and to the financial services legislative proposals in the future. our rapporteur, mr villiers, who has done an excellent job, has in his report raised the possibility of using the so-called fast-track procedure. i fully support him in this idea, as i believe it is important that the financial services action plan is implemented as quickly as possible. in practice, the only way to do this is to speed up the legislative procedure and to allow more cases to be dealt with under the urgent procedure. - mr president, the office of european ombudsman was created by the maastricht treaty at the same time as the concept of citizenship of the union was introduced. the ombudsman is a lobbyist for the interests of the citizens against the administration. the ombudsman, mr jacob söderman, works in accordance with his mandate to represent the people and to promote good governance in the union. we all know there's still a lot of work to be done. however, the adoption of good, transparent and efficient governance is essential if the european union is to win the support and confidence of its citizens. this time, the ombudsman's report on the secrecy surrounding the commission's recruitment procedure is on the agenda. the report is accompanied by recommendations which the commission has already started to implement. in the explanatory memorandum to his report, herbert bösch, rapporteur for the european parliament's committee on petitions, states that the principles which led to the ombudsman's recommendations proved to be the same as those followed by the european parliament, namely correctness, transparency and the desire to inspire confidence. it's the way it should be. parliament is a body directly elected by the people. it is responsible for overseeing the functioning of the union's civil service on behalf of the citizens, in addition to legislation. parliament and the ombudsman must therefore be on the same page. the report by the committee on petitions states that the committee approves the special report under consideration and in particular the section on improving the impression of the institutions on citizens in tendering procedures. the main point of the ombudsman's proposals concerns the right of applicants to have their corrected test papers - mr president, the main objective of the nice summit is to enlarge the european union and to prepare the union for this enlargement. i can also say on behalf of my group that we are fully committed to this enlargement being as soon as possible. but it is important to discuss what will happen after nice. this calls for visions, and these visions have been presented in the last few months. our group strongly supports the constitutionalisation of the treaties of the european union, which also means that the reform of the treaties is open and that civil society can also participate in this process. we consider the bill of rights a success. of course, the text could have been better, and it can be refined and clarified after nice. it will no doubt become part of the constitution of the european union in due course. but we would also like to see at least a reference to it in article 6 of the treaty on european union, because citizens should already be benefiting from this charter of fundamental rights. as regards the decisions which are now closer to us, we cannot stress enough the importance of qualified majority voting. the nice summit cannot succeed unless more qualified majority votes are obtained. it is very important that the european parliament is closely involved in the codecision procedure, otherwise legislative decisions will be even more difficult to understand; the european parliament brings transparency to this decision-making. let us take environmental taxes, for example: we often criticise the united states for not wanting to give up its sovereignty and commit itself to international agreements on climate protection when discussing climate policy, but there are member states among us who also adhere to their imaginary southern border and do not want to go to qualified majority decisions, for example on environmental taxation. we have to get rid of this. president prodi spoke of trade policy. we all want the european union to play a strong role in a globalising world, but mr prodi, our group demands that trade policy should be open and transparent as a condition for the commission to have more powers, and that is certainly not the case at the moment. finally, i would also like to say that it is important at this time to ensure that the european union does not become a community of large member states only. one of the things that has been a problem in parliament is that we should be aware that the future size of parliament cannot be categorically limited to seven hundred, because that also limits the diversity that is going to be manifested in parliament. it is causing a shift towards a two-party system which cannot be democratic. i would ask mr barón crespo to understand this, because i have the feeling that the other presidents will support this objective of keeping the whole of the european parliament somewhat open. this would be a very important position for parliament to take and we can vote on it tomorrow.
- mr president, i shall concentrate my speech on mrs lalumière's report, which i think is well written and clear. the decision taken at the helsinki summit to set up an eu rapid response force has been interpreted in different ways in the member states. in finland and sweden, crisis management is used to refer to traditional peacekeeping operations. mrs lalumière's report reflects the thinking of many nato countries that the rapid reaction forces will develop into a full-fledged military force very quickly. the equipment would include heavy transport aircraft, aircraft carriers, cruise missiles, and refueling aircraft for long-range bombers. finns have never been told anything like this. i believe that the european military identity should have been created within nato, in which case the eu would have been limited to crisis management. i cannot accept the development of the eu into a military alliance and a military superpower. now that the development of the eu's military dimension has been launched, it is wise to seek to differentiate the union internally in this area as well. the nato countries could form a defence community within the union, which would act as a strong european pillar of nato. in this case, the other eu member states could stick to traditional peacekeeping. i cannot accept some of the details in mrs lalumière's report. in the final vote i shall support the rejection of the report.
- mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank mrs lalumière for her very well-researched report. i would like to address two points: the union's civil crisis management is not developed, conflicts are social, economic, industrial, ecological and ethnic in nature. in all of them, preventive civil crisis management is a better option than military. my fear is that rapid intervention will increasingly be used in place of civilian crisis management, and increasingly at a faster rate. the european flag is not being carried by a tank, but by social equality, and there is a fundamental difference. i propose that we organize a civilian crisis management center as efficient and fast as the one now planned. then we will act in the right order and we will implement our own philosophy correctly. the second fear is that countries that do not want to join nato will become involved. there is one solution that could make this easier: involving russia in the crisis management. it is clear that russia is often either involved or otherwise involved in the crises we are facing. we also know that president putin is not strongly opposed to these crisis management forces, which means that i think there is scope for cooperation, and once we know what kind of crises are involved, i think this would be another balancing element in the whole process. it would make it easier for the peoples of europe to understand this whole issue.
- mr president, first of all i would like to thank all four rapporteurs for their good work. improving the governance of the eu is the most important reform of this parliamentary term. the main objective of the commission's reform should be to reduce bureaucracy, increase efficiency and remove financial uncertainties. this requires simplification of working methods, the devolution of powers and the clarification of decision-making powers. the key to reform is to increase and emphasize personal responsibility. the heads of the institutions must bear both legal and financial responsibility for their decisions. the emphasis on personal responsibility requires, among other things, a rapid reform of the staff regulations. the reform requires activity-based budgeting. the heads of departments and agencies must have a certain degree of flexibility in their management. the main emphasis must be on the results to be achieved and not on the way of working. only in this way can the resources available be allocated more effectively. the institution and its head must have a clear possibility of the best possible governance. the transfer of responsibility requires the abandonment of ex ante controls on financing. if there is more than one decision-maker, no one has a clear responsibility. the reform of the administration is necessary in particular to improve the european union's monetary system. the eu's bills must be paid in the same way as in the outside world. the current three-month payment period is too long, not to mention that many payments are delayed for more than a year. it is shameful, given that the eu itself is the champion of modernising the economy. my group strongly supports the reform of the administration.
i too would like to thank the rapporteurs for their successful reports. they have addressed the issues that are essential to enhance maritime safety. however, much remains to be done to improve maritime safety. as regards port control, it is important to further tighten the rules and to increase the effectiveness of controls. the approval and supervision of classification societies must also be improved, and the expanded inspections must be specifically targeted at ships at risk. i would also like to stress that the solutions to improve maritime safety within the union alone are not enough. for example, liability issues must be addressed on a global basis. this also applies to dual cell requirements. regional regulation is not enough here either, but global agreements within the imo are needed. for example, it is essential for finland that russia is involved in these agreements. in the case of unseaworthy and risky vessels, care must be taken to ensure that they are actually scrapped and not put into service under another flag.
mrs chairman, a series of high-profile ship disasters, particularly involving oil tankers, has hit our continent in the recent past. tremendous damage has been caused to nature as well as to the livelihoods of the population living in these areas. our electors ask us what we are doing to prevent catastrophes like these? i would like to thank ms wallström and ms de palacio for the action they have taken in the field of maritime safety. they definitely have reacted quickly and worked swiftly for better rules and legislation regarding safety at sea in both the ‘erika i’ and ‘erika ii’ proposals. the phasing out of single-hull oil tankers will increase safety along eu coasts. my friends in the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism remarked that stricter eu phase-out dates should be adopted. i thank my colleagues for this responsible approach and i urge agreement to be reached on the earlier drop-dead date of 2015. when we do this we should always remember that double hulls or even triple hulls do not guarantee with absolute certainty that accidents will not happen in the future. presenting new technologies and making improvements to the vessel design are part of this important work. as regards the commission decision yesterday to stick with the previously agreed timetable regarding the state aid schemes i think that was a wise decision. like my very good friend mrs langenhagen i am very worried about the situation in south korea and i was glad to see that the european commission will review this issue before may next year.

mr president, we all know that this is an extremely important report. it is important because the credibility of the european union's public health policy is now being weighed. i regret that there have also been views in the committee on legal affairs and the internal market that the court's ban on tobacco advertising would in some way invalidate this directive. no, this directive does not concern tobacco content or measures to promote tobacco. we should no longer be misleading this parliament on this matter, but we should understand that it is now important to defend the very meritorious report by the rapporteur, mr maatene. what about the fact that the light and mild labels should now be banned? we should insist that such misleading labels cannot be allowed and the court's decision in october does not add anything new to this. we must stick to the position we have taken in the past. it is now necessary to realize that in all those countries where it has been agreed to draw up relatively strict tobacco legislation, the mortality rate from lung cancer and cardiovascular disease has also been clearly controlled. we should also be able to promote all this in the european union's common internal market policy, which must be based on a high level of protection of public health.
mr president, summertime has been used in several member states since the 1970s. the start and end dates of summertime were finally harmonised a few years ago, so that it has been uniform in all eu member states since 1996. the practice has become commonplace, as the previous speaker has just said. the proposal that has now been put forward is good in the view of our group and our group supports it and no further amendments are needed. the key point is that summertime is implemented in all member states and that the start and end dates of summertime are still kept uniform. i think it is good that the question of time zones and the application of summertime arrangements should be left to the member states, as proposed. the harmonization is therefore only necessary in the context of practical implementation, i.e. that summertime is actually implemented in all member states at the same time. a uniform summer-time schedule will remove obstacles to the free movement of goods, services and persons and will contribute to the more effective completion of the internal market. studies on the effects of summertime do not give a clear picture of whether the effects on agriculture, for example, are negative or positive. the main concerns are related to animal welfare and working conditions for farmers. the introduction of daylight saving time has led to energy savings, although the savings reported in the report are very small. studies have shown that road safety has improved with increased night-time light. longer, brighter evenings also provide opportunities for increased leisure time.

mr president, we must work with russia, which is politically wounded, to make russia a european state with a desire for culture and civilization. the report by mr oostlander is critical and cooperative. we must work together to heal the wounds inflicted by ten years of brutal capitalism in russia. boycotts won't bite. russia is in a bad way right now. there is no middle class, only super rich and super poor. half the population lives below the poverty line. moral decay is rampant, almost like sodom and gomorrah. the number of hiv-positive people is feared to approach 600,000 in the coming years, and the disease is also striking the young. 80% of the current classes are under 25 years old. the birth rate is falling and the death rate is rising, and russia's net population loss is one million people a year. it's a sign of people not feeling well. the earth's burden is also to protect nature and people from the mistakes of the past. the anthrax bacteria we've developed for biological warfare has a capacity of 300 tons, and even a small dose of it is lethal. as a result of the disarmament negotiations, tens of tons of waste plutonium will have to be disposed of. the eu must help russia, because it is also doing so to counter its own risks. the main area of cooperation is energy policy, which involves great power politics, for example, the way in which oil and gas are transported from east to west. today, already 30 to 40% of the eu's gas comes from russia, and in 10 years' time the eu will be almost completely dependent on russian gas. the eu must not be a geopolitical extension of the us in gas and oil pipelines. the threat to the russians is not the eu, but a world with a single leader, the united states. the russians are not yet able to oppose the insidious expansion of nato through the militarization of eu structures. we must not be cunning, but must build a europe of economic cooperation.

, draftsman of the opinion of the committee on budgets. mr president, the digital content industry is characterised by an increase in the amount of information. information is the raw material of information in the content industry, and multimedia is also fully exploiting the potential of the entertainment and leisure industries to increase the volume of content production. there are no longer the same limits to the transmission of digital information. digital content, which is more about quantity than quality, is a homogenizing and typifying of cultural and social diversity. 70% of the internet content is in english, but the challenge for europe is to make better use of the linguistic wealth. although linguistic differences may cause additional costs, multilingual content is an important prerequisite for democracy in the eu. to overcome these challenges, a language industry focusing on linguistic tailoring and diversifying the language infrastructure of digital content is needed. the same applies to the localisation of content production, which is the adaptation of products to the linguistic and cultural needs and preferences of consumers. in the content industry, maximising volume may come at the expense of linguistically valuable content, both culturally and socially. therefore, public support measures for the eu content industry must also aim at achieving a high level of literacy and a diverse culture. in the modern age, it's not enough for people to be able to read, media literacy is really going to be as important in the new millennium as traditional literacy. in the committee on budgets we have paid attention to the importance of content production and the exploitation of public information and have considered the budgetary resources allocated to this purpose to be very limited. but we think this is a good start. we also thank mrs gill for her active and decisive role in formulating the substance of parliament's position.

mr president, on behalf of the socialist group i should like to thank the rapporteur. we can agree with him on many points and we are satisfied with the outcome of the committee's work. the united nations has declared the year of older persons, and it has been very important that attention be paid in all parts of the world to the future development of the situation of older people in countries such as the european union. i think it is of paramount importance that older people are seen as independent people and that their own actions are supported as far as possible in their lives. we have the same problems or the same prospects for the future in all the countries of the european union. we are faced with the question of how to keep older people in the labour market and what this means for the labour market. on the other hand, we see that as the population ages and ages in better health than ever before, the desire to leave the labour market earlier increases. this must take account of how the labour market works for different people. how do we support older people to survive longer and longer in the labour market? how do we organise lifelong learning so that older people also have the new knowledge, new skills and also the motivation to remain more active in working life? so there are a number of complex issues that need to be considered in this matter. i would like to make a special point about some of the aspects of aging women. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the social partners are able to take part in the negotiations, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent report. even if we were to achieve the same pay for the same work in the long term, we must remember that women need special treatment on the social security side because they have different working histories, and that needs to be taken into account because it will then be reflected, for example, in future pensions.
mr president, the committee on employment and social affairs has produced an important report on the commission's communication. when the market is only interested in young people and young people's purchasing power, the issues of older people remain in the realm of politics. in many eu countries, there is debate about whether pension rights and benefits acquired can be safeguarded in the future. in many countries, pensions have been promised without adequate funds being raised to pay them. when it comes to the single currency, the euro, and monetary policy is the same in all countries, these countries are under particularly strong downward pressure on pensions. the pension commitments must be kept, but the pensions must be secured from the resources of these countries without having to secure the payment of pensions at the expense of the eu's common monetary policy. the security of the elderly and of the elderly citizen is most assured by a secure livelihood. the right to work is also one of the fundamental human rights. and the sbarbat report rightly draws attention to age discrimination and ageism in working life. political parties must ensure equality in employment, the market cannot.
